The FBSDE approach to sine–Gordon up to 6 ​ � 6\pi
Massimiliano Gubinelli, Sarah-Jean Meyer

Abstract
We develop a stochastic analysis of the sine-Gordon Euclidean quantum field ( cos ⁡ ( � ​ ' ) ) 2 (\cos(\beta\varphi))_{2} on the full space up to the second threshold, i.e. for � 2 < 6 ​ � \beta^{2}<6\pi . The basis of our method is a forward-backward stochastic differential equation (FBSDE) for a decomposition ( X t ) t ¾ 0 (X_{t})_{t\geqslant 0} of the interacting Euclidean field X ∞ X_{\infty} along a scale parameter t ¾ 0 t\geqslant 0 . This FBSDE describes the optimiser of the stochastic control representation of the Euclidean QFT introduced by Barashkov and one of the authors. We show that the FBSDE provides a description of the interacting field without cut-offs and that it can be used effectively to study the sine-Gordon measure to obtain results about large deviations, sub-gaussian tails, decay of correlations for local observables, singularity with respect to the free field, Osterwalder–Schrader axioms and other properties.



keywords

stochastic quantisation;
forward-backward stochastic differential equations;
Euclidean quantum field theory;
sine-Gordon model

MSC (2020).
Primary 81S20; Secondary 60H30

1
Introduction

The aim of this paper is to provide a rigorous
description
of the
two-dimensional sine-Gordon Euclidean quantum field theory (EQFT) on the full
space in the regime
\beta^{2}<6\pi
. The sine-Gordon EQFT is formally given
by the Gibbs measure

\text{``}\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=\Xi^{-1}\exp(-V_{\operatorname{SG}}(\varphi))\mu(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\text{{\phantom{.}}''},\quad\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2}),

(1.1)

where
\mu
is a massive Gaussian free field on the space of Schwartz
distributions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
, the constant
\Xi
is a
normalisation to make
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
a probability measure, and

V_{\operatorname{SG}}
corresponds to the cosine interaction, formally defined as

V_{\operatorname{SG}}(\varphi):=\lambda\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\cos(\beta\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}x.

The sine-Gordon model is a prototypical example of a non-Gaussian EQFT and of
particular interest as both a theory with infinitely many phase transitions as

\beta^{2}
varies between
0
and
8\pi
and more generally as a test-bed for
non-polynomial interactions.

The approach we take here is based on a scale dependent interpolation

(G_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty]}
of the covariance
G_{\infty}=(\Delta-m^{2})^{-1}
of the Gaussian free field. This allows us to interpret the Gaussian free
field as the terminal value
W_{\infty}
of a Brownian martingale
(W_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
defined by

W_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\dot{G}_{s}^{1/2}\mathrm{d}B_{s},\qquad t\geqslant 0,

(1.2)

where
B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is a cylindrical Brownian motion on
L^{2}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
. From this point of view, we can produce a scale dependent
stochastic dynamics
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty]}
for the target measure
(
1.1
) which provides a pathwise
scale-by-scale coupling
(X_{t},W_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty]}
.

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\dot{G}_{t}\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\mathrm{D}V_{\operatorname{SG}}(X_{\infty})]\mathrm{d}t+\dot{G}_{t}^{1/2}\mathrm{d}B_{t},\qquad t\geqslant 0.

(1.3)

Here,
\mathrm{D}V_{\operatorname{SG}}(\varphi)=-\lambda\beta\sin(\beta\varphi)
is
formally the functional derivative of the interaction potential

V_{\operatorname{SG}}
, and we write
\dot{G}_{t}:=\partial_{t}G_{t}
for the scale
derivative of
G
. The conditional expectation
\mdmathbb E_{t}
is taken with respect to the
augmentation of the filtration generated by the Brownian motion
W
and can
be interpreted as averaging out the small scales
s^{-1}\leqslant t^{-1}
.

In this paper, we show that, once properly renormalised, the
FBSDE (
1.3
) provides an effective stochastic quantisation
equation for (
1.1
) for any size of the coupling constant
\bar{\lambda}:=|\lambda|\in\mdmathbb{R}
. This allows to construct the measure
(
1.1
) without cut-offs from a straightforward analysis of the
equation and only basic estimates of the convolution
\dot{G}
(see
Theorem
1.1
). Moreover, we can efficiently transport properties
from the Gaussian free field to the sine-Gordon EQFT via
(
1.3
). In particular, we obtain

a)

an explicit description of the infinite volume measure via a
variational principle (Theorem
7.7
);

b)

a proof of the mutual singularity of the Gaussian free field and the
finite volume sine-Gordon measure for
\beta^{2}\geqslant 4\pi
(Theorem
1.4
);

c)

a simple proof for the exponential decay of correlations of general
local observables (Theorem
1.3
);

d)

an analysis of the semi-classical limit
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0
(Theorem
1.5
);

e)

a full verification of the Osterwalder–Schrader axioms and a proof of
non-Gaussianity (Theorem
8.1
);

In order to give a rigorous meaning
to (
1.3
) we start, as usual, from a well-defined
approximation of the sine-Gordon measure given by

\nu^{\rho,T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=\Xi_{\rho,T}^{-1}\exp(-V^{\rho,T}(\varphi))\mu^{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi),

(1.4)

where
\rho
is an infrared cut-off and
\mu^{T}
denotes the law of an
approximation
W_{T}
to the massive Gaussian free field
W_{\infty}
as
in (
1.2
). In Section
2
we will show that in this
regularised setting, the FBSDE (
1.3
) produces the correct
measure, that is the solution
(X_{t})_{t}
to the FBSDE

\mathrm{d}X^{\rho,T}_{t}=-\dot{G}_{t}\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\mathrm{D}V^{\rho,T}(X^{\rho,T}_{T})]\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W_{t},\qquad t\in[0,T]

(1.5)

has terminal law
\operatorname{Law}(X_{T}^{\rho,T})=\nu^{\rho,T}
. As a byproduct, we show
that it is associated with the solution to the stochastic optimal control
problem

-\log\mdmathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{-V^{\rho,T}(W_{T})}]=\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}\mdmathbb{E}\left[V^{\rho,T}(I_{T}(u)+W_{T})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|u_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}t\right],

(1.6)

where
I_{t}(u):=\int_{0}^{t}\dot{G}^{1/2}_{s}u_{s}\mathrm{d}s
and

\mdmathbb{H}_{a}
is an appropriate space of predictable processes. As expected,
the representations (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) are not
stable in the small-scale limit
T\rightarrow\infty
, and they require a
renormalisation of the potential
V^{\rho,T}
involving diverging constants.
To overcome this problem, suppose that
F
is a sufficiently nice scale
dependent function
F=(F_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
such that
F_{T}=\mathrm{D}V^{\rho,T}
. By Ito’s formula, solving the FBSDE (
1.5
) is equivalent
to solving the FBSDE

\begin{cases}Z_{t}=-\int_{0}^{t}\dot{G}_{s}(F_{s}(Z_{s}+W_{s})+R_{s})\mathrm{d}s,\\
R_{t}=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}H_{s}(Z_{s}+W_{s})\mathrm{d}s-\mdmathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{D}F_{s}\dot{G}_{s}R_{s}\mathrm{d}s,\end{cases}\qquad t\in[0,T],

(1.7)

where the functional
(H_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is given by

H_{t}:=\partial_{t}F_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}^{2}F_{t})-\mathrm{D}F_{t}\dot{G}_{t}F_{t},\qquad t\in[0,T].

The solution
X
to (
1.5
) can then be obtained
from (
1.7
) with the identification
X_{t}=Z_{t}+W_{t}
. In this
representation, the limit
T\rightarrow\infty
is associated to the
convergence of the integral over scales in the equation for the

remainder

R
. Constructing the measure (
1.1
)
reduces to two tasks:

1.

Find an approximation
F
for the effective force
\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\mathrm{D}V_{\operatorname{SG}}(X_{\infty})]
that makes the source term
H_{s}(W_{s}+Z_{s})
of the backward equation in (
1.7
) integrable as
s\rightarrow\infty
, while preserving good continuity and growth properties.

2.

Control the associated FBSDE (
1.7
) uniformly in the
regularisations
T
and
\rho
and establish global existence for the
solutions to (
1.7
).

The first task involves a good understanding of approximate solutions to the
well-known infinite dimensional and non-linear (backward) Polchinski renormalisation flow
equation (see
e.g.
[
36
]
or the recent
review
[
10
]
),

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}^{2}v_{t})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}v_{t}\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}v_{t}=0,\\
v_{\infty}(\varphi)=V_{\operatorname{SG}}(\varphi).\end{cases}

(1.8)

Indeed, given a solution
v
to (
1.8
) and taking
F_{t}=\mathrm{D}v_{t}
we would have
H_{t}=0
and therefore
R_{t}=0
. The remainder
R

allows for additional freedom in the choice for the scale interpolation of the
force
F_{t}
and avoids a precise technical analysis of (
1.8
).

The second task requires good a priori estimates for the non-standard FBSDE
(
1.7
), which are uniform in the regularisation
T
. The
equation (
1.7
) is in general nonlinear and thus solutions need
not be global so that this step is non-trivial and indeed the reason why the present
work is limited to the regime
\beta^{2}<6\pi
. It would be very interesting
to better understand the solution theory for FBSDEs of the form
(
1.7
) also in a more general setting for different models, that
is different choices of
F_{t}
.

Our main result is the following.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\beta^{2}<6\pi
. For
\rho\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
or
\rho\equiv 1
and
T\in[0,\infty]
, there is scale
dependent function
F^{\rho,T}=(F^{\rho,T}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
such that
up to first order (in
\bar{\lambda}
),

F^{\rho,T}_{T}
corresponds perturbatively to the Wick-renormalised sine

F^{\rho,T}_{t}(W_{t\wedge T})(x)=-\rho(x)\beta\lambda\llbracket\sin(\beta W_{t\wedge T}(x))\rrbracket+O(\bar{\lambda}^{2}),

and the associated FBSDE (
1.7
) has a solution
(Z^{\rho,T},R^{\rho,T})\in\mdmathbb{H}^{\infty}(L^{\infty})\times\mdmathbb{H}^{\infty}(L^{\infty})
.

If the volume is finite, that is
\rho\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
, or if the coupling constant
\bar{\lambda}
is sufficiently small,
\operatorname{Law}(X_{\infty}^{\rho})=\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}
is unique. For
\rho=1,T=\infty
and any
\varepsilon>0
,
there is a version of the drift
Z=Z^{1,\infty}
with terminal value

Z_{\infty}\in L^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}\mdmathbb{P};B_{p,p}^{2-\beta^{2}/4\pi-\varepsilon,-n})
, and the sine-Gordon measure is given as a random shift
of the Gaussian free field
W_{\infty}
,

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}=\operatorname{Law}(W_{\infty}+Z_{\infty}).

It should be emphasised that while our analysis provides uniqueness only if
the coupling constant
\bar{\lambda}
is small or the volume is finite, its
existence is guaranteed for any
\lambda\in\mdmathbb{R}
also in the full
space: we obtain uniform bounds on the FBSDE for any
\lambda\in\mdmathbb{R}

which imply tightness for the family
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T}=\operatorname{Law}(W_{T}+Z^{\rho,T}_{T})
. Indeed, at the level of the approximate measures (
1.4
), Theorem
1.1
implies following result.

Corollary 1.2
.

For any
\lambda\in\mdmathbb R
, the family of measures
(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T})_{T,\rho}
defined in (
1.4
) is tight on
H^{-\varepsilon}(\langle x\rangle^{-2})
. Moreover, there is a unique accumulation point if either of the conditions below is satisfied

•

\bar{\lambda}
is sufficiently small. In this case
(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T})_{T,\rho}
converges
H^{-\varepsilon}(\langle x\rangle^{-2})
-weakly to a measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
.

•

\rho\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
remains fixed. Then, as
T\to\infty
, the sequence
(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T})_{T\geqslant 0}
converges
H^{-\varepsilon}(\langle x\rangle^{-2})
-weakly to a limiting measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}
.

To demonstrate the advantages of the representation, we transport some
properties of the free field
W_{\infty}
to the sine-Gordon shift
W_{\infty}+Z_{\infty}
. A neat application is the exponential decay of correlation via
a simple coupling argument as
in
[
39
,
27
]
.
In this setting, we can show that for the unique solution
Z_{t}
to
(
1.7
) at
T=\infty
,
\rho=1
, the process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty]}=(Z_{t}+W_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty]}
inherits the following decay
of correlations from
W
. Note that the theorem below includes
t=\infty

and thus
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}=\operatorname{Law}(X_{\infty})
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\chi
be a smooth function supported
on
B_{1}(0)
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{2}
. If
\bar{\lambda}
is sufficiently small,
then there is a constant

\gamma\in(0,1)
depending only on the mass
m
such that for any two
bounded and Lipschitz observables
\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}:H^{-\varepsilon,-n}\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
, it holds that

\left|\mathrm{Cov}[\mathcal{O}_{1}(\chi\cdot X_{t}(\cdot+x_{1}));\mathcal{O}_{2}(\chi\cdot X_{t}(\cdot+x_{2}))]\right|\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-m\gamma|x_{1}-x_{2}|}.

Here, the implicit constant depends only on the bounds and Lipschitz
constants of the observables
\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{2}
.

In the first region
\beta^{2}<4\pi
, it is not difficult to see that the
finite volume sine-Gordon measure is absolutely continuous with respect to the
Gaussian free field. Using the FBSDE, we
can show that this is no longer the case beyond this threshold. To the best of
our knowledge, this is the first proof of this fact.

Theorem 1.4
.

For
\beta^{2}\in[4\pi,6\pi)
, the finite volume
sine-Gordon measure and the Gaussian free field are mutually singular.

As a result of this singularity, the control problem (
1.6
) cannot
be transferred to the UV-limit verbatim, in contrast to the simpler setting

\beta^{2}<4\pi
(treated in
[
9
]
).
Building on the same ideas used for the change of variables in the FBSDE from
(
1.3
) to (
1.7
), we reformulate the variational
problem (
1.6
) in terms of an (absolutely continuous) remainder.
This reformulation, combined with a localisation property of the limiting
measure, allows us to recover a variational problem for the Laplace transform
of
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
in the infinite volume (see
7.7
) as long as
\bar{\lambda}
is sufficiently small.

With this variational formulation at hand, we can show that the
limiting measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
defines a non-Gaussian EQFT and derive a
Laplace principle for the semi-classical limit
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0
. To make
this slightly more precise, let
(\mu^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}})_{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\in(0,1)}
be the
family of rescaled Gaussian free fields with covariance
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}
. For now, formally define the measures

\text{``}\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi):=\Xi_{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}^{-1}\exp(-\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{-1}V(\varphi))\mu^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\text{.''}

We show the following theorem.

Theorem 1.5
.

As
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0
, the family

\nu^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\operatorname{SG}}
satisfies a Laplace principle with rate
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{-1}
and rate function

I(\varphi):=\begin{cases}\lambda\int(\cos(\beta\varphi)-1)+\frac{1}{2}\int\varphi(m^{2}-\Delta)\varphi,&\varphi\in H^{1}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2}),\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.9)

More precisely, for any continuous and bounded
f:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
,

\lim_{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0}-\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\log\int_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})}\exp(-\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{-1}f(\varphi))\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=\inf_{\varphi\in H^{1}}\{f(\varphi)+I(\varphi)\}.

(1.10)

Finally, we verify the
Osterwalder Schrader axioms for small couplings
\bar{\lambda}
.

Remark 1.6
.

Our approach relies only on some general estimates for the heat kernel of
the Laplacian (see Appendix
A
) and can be easily extended with
respect to the dimension of the underlying Euclidean space. In the general

d
-dimensional setting, the sine-Gordon theory can be considered with respect to the
d
-dimensional log-correlated Gaussian field and is subcritical for
\beta^{2}/2\pi\in[0,2d)
and the argument presented here allows to construct the
sine-Gordon measure in
\beta^{2}/2\pi\in[0,d+1)
. In one dimension, this means we can cover the full subcritical
regime, (see also

[
31
]
, where a one-dimensional version of the model is considered). We can moreover generalise our results to the
(compact) Riemannian manifold context, in analogy to the recent
work
[
2
]
on
\Phi^{4}_{3}
on a compact
Riemannian manifold. For the sake of clarity, we refrain from including
these modifications. The required changes are minimal and we do not believe
that the associated results would justify extending this contribution.

1.1
Related work

The sine–Gordon model has been subject to many studies in the constructive
literature, covering finite or infinite volume interactions and allowing
various ranges for
\beta^{2}\in[0,8\pi)
and the coupling constant
\lambda\in\mdmathbb{R}
. However, the full mathematical understanding of this model is
still lacking and none of these works cover the theory on the full space

\mdmathbb{R}^{2}
for all
\beta^{2}\in(0,8\pi)
and all
\lambda\in\mdmathbb{R}
. A comprehensive review of the vast literature on the model can be found in the
paper
[
13
]
where in the reader
will also find a description of the correspondence with certain fermionic
Euclidean models.

We single out the pioneering work of Benfatto et
al.
[
14
]
and Nicoló et
al.
[
32
]
who establish existence of
the model for a finite volume interaction and small coupling constants in the
full subcritical range
\beta^{2}<8\pi
via a probabilistic method initiated
by the Roman school of Gallavotti and co-authors. A more modern account is the
martingale method of
[
31
]
which
covers the full subcritical regime in the case
d=1
, in a bounded domain
but without restrictions on the coupling constant
\lambda\in\mdmathbb{R}
.

The more classical stochastic quantisation using the Langevin dynamics for the
sine-Gordon model has only been partially resolved after the preprint of
the present article appeared. In contrast to the
\Phi^{4}_{3}
theory, there is no
obvious coercive term to ensure global in time existence and even though the
local solution theory was established already in

[
29
,
20
]
,
the first global in time existence results for
4\pi+\varepsilon
were obtained by Chandra, Feltes and
Weber
[
19
]
as a corollary of global existence for the generalised parabolic Anderson model in a finite volume
(see also the related paper
[
37
]
).
Even more recently, Bringmann and Cao
[
17
]

managed to show global well-posedness for the Langevin dynamics on the torus for
\beta^{2}<6\pi
.

Due to the analytic treatability of the sine-Gordon interaction, there have
been several accounts based on renormalisation group ideas and a direct
analysis of the Polchinski flow equation (
1.8
). In this
regard, we want to mention the analysis of Brydges and
Kennedy
[
18
]
, where they lay the
foundations for this approach relying on a majorant method to establish
convergence of the Mayer expansion up to
\beta^{2}<\frac{2}{3}8\pi
. More
recently, Bauerschmidt and
Bodineau
[
11
]
showed
convergence for the Mayer expansion up to
6\pi
which allows them to
establish a uniform log-Sobolev inequality for a lattice approximation of the
model. In a related work, Bauerschmidt and
Hofstetter
[
12
]
use the solution
obtained from the Mayer expansion to construct a multiscale coupling between
the Gaussian free field and the sine-Gordon model and analyse the maximum of
the sine-Gordon measure. Similar ideas were applied by Barashkov, Gunaratnam
and Hofstetter
[
7
]
to analyse the
maximum of the
P(\varphi)_{2}
models in a bounded domain. These last two
papers are similar in spirit and complementary to ours, but rely on a direct
analysis of the Polchinski equation (
1.8
) and focus on the
extremal analysis in a finite volume instead of a general analysis and
properties of the resulting EQFT.

Focusing now on the connection between the FBSDE and stochastic optimal
control, a direct precursor of the results presented here is the work of
Barashkov
[
9
]
(and the related PhD
thesis
[
8
]
), where the model is
studied in the first region
\beta^{2}<4\pi
on the full space
\mdmathbb{R}^{2}

using a variational approach. This approach is based on the stochastic
control problem (
1.6
) and was first applied to the
\Phi^{4}_{3}

model in bounded volume in
[
4
]
. The
more recent extension in
[
6
]
to
the infinite volume limit for the polynomial and exponential interaction in
the
2
dimensional setting relies on a weak formulation of the FBSDE we use
here. In the case of a Grassmannian field, the FBSDE approach has been
successfully applied in
[
39
]
to
cover the full subcritical regime. This also includes the complete inductive
analysis of the corresponding approximate flow equation. After a first preprint
of this article got published, Duch constructed a marginal asymptotically free Fermionic theory

[
22
]
by solving the continuous renormalisation
group exactly.

Finally, we want to point out a general (tentative) axiomatic
framework
[
3
]
proposed by Bailleul,
Chevyrev and the first author. This framework provides a generalisation of
the coupling with the free field given by (
1.3
) to the
construction of random fields endowed with a Wilsonian scale-decomposition and a
stochastic dynamics associated to a Gaussian field. These so called
Wilson–Itô fields generate interesting questions ranging from the
characterisation of measures of the form (
1.1
) via FBSDEs,
to locality properties, the structure of the pre-factorisation algebras
generated by the observables, or generalisations of the domain Markov
properties, some of which we hope to address in a future study.

1.2
Notation and assumptions

Let us fix some general notation we will use throughout.

•

Let
\langle x\rangle:=(1+|x|^{2})^{1/2}
,
x\in\mdmathbb{R}^{2}
. We will often rely on the following inequality to commute
polynomial weights,

\langle x\rangle^{k}\langle y\rangle^{-k}\lesssim\langle x-y\rangle^{k},\quad k\in\mdmathbb{N}.

(1.11)

•

For
\gamma\in(-1,1)
, we define the exponential weights

w(x):=\mathrm{e}^{\gamma m|x|}.

•

For a non-negative function
w:\mdmathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}_{+}
, we define
the standard weighted Lebesgue, Sobolev and Besov spaces
L^{p}(w)
,
W^{s,p}(w),H^{s}(w)=W^{s,2}(w)
and
B^{s}_{p,q}(w)
,
p,q\in[1,\infty],s\in\mdmathbb{R}
based on the measures
w(x)\mathrm{d}x
on
\mdmathbb{R}^{2}
,
e.g.
L^{p}(w)
is equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{L^{p}(w)}^{p}=\|w\cdot f\|_{L^{p}}^{p}=\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}|fw|^{p}=\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}|w(x)f(x)|^{p}\mathrm{d}x.

In the case of
w(x)=\langle x\rangle^{k}
for some
k\in\mdmathbb{R}
, we
also write
L^{p,k}:=L^{p}(\langle x\rangle^{k})
and analogously for
the Besov and Sobolev spaces. Throughout this paper, we will fix
a polynomial weight with
n
sufficiently large so that
x\mapsto\langle x\rangle^{-n}\in L^{1}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
.

•

We denote by
\Delta_{i}=\varphi_{i}(\mathrm{D})
the Littlewood-Paley
blocks on
\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
and by
K_{i}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\varphi_{i})
their
associated
L^{p}
-kernels. We recall that then, for any
i\geqslant-1
and

p\in[1,\infty)
,

\|K_{i}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim 1,\quad\|K_{i}\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim 2^{2i\frac{p-1}{p}}.

(1.12)

For any
\alpha\in\mdmathbb{R}
,
p,q\in[1,\infty]
and
n
we define
the usual Besov norms (with the usual modification for
q=\infty
)

\|u\|^{q}_{B_{p,q}^{\alpha}(\langle x\rangle^{-n})}:=\sum_{i\geqslant-1}2^{\alpha iq}\|\Delta_{i}u\|^{q}_{L^{p,-n}},

with the corresponding Besov spaces

B^{\alpha}_{p,q}(\langle x\rangle^{-n}):=\left\{u\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{d}):\>\|u\|_{B^{\alpha}_{p,q}(\langle x\rangle^{-n})}<\infty\right\}.

For a
more detailed exposition, we refer to
[
1
, Chapter
1]
.

•

For a collection of points
x_{I}=(x_{i})_{i\in I}
we denote its

Steiner diameter
, that is the shortest tree connecting all points
in
x_{I}
, by
\operatorname{St}(x_{I})
. More precisely, we define

\operatorname{St}(x_{I}):=\min_{x_{J}\supset x_{I}}\min_{\tau(x_{J})}L(\tau),

(1.13)

where the second minimum runs over all trees
\tau(x_{J})
connecting the
points
x_{J}
and
L(\tau)
measures the length of the tree
\tau
on

\mdmathbb{R}^{d}
. We refer to
[
25
]

for further details.

•

We denote by
B=(B_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
a cylindrical Brownian motion
on
L^{2}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
and by
\mdmathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}

the augmentation of the filtration generated by
B
. All considerations are
with respect to this filtration and we will not explicitly mention it
elsewhere (i.e. adapted always means adapted to the filtration

\mdmathbb{F}
). The conditional expectation with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t}

is denoted by
\mdmathbb{E}_{t}
. Given a measure
\nu
, we
write
\nu(f):=\int f\mathrm{d}\nu
for the expectation under this
measure and if
\nu
is a probability measure, we write

\operatorname{Cov}_{\nu}(f,g):=\nu(fg)-\nu(f)\nu(g).

•

For a Banach space
\mathcal{X}
, let
\mdmathbb{H}_{a}(\mathcal{X})
be
the space of predictable processes taking values in
\mathcal{X}
(no
integrability restrictions assumed). We also define the spaces, for any
p\in[1,\infty]

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{H}^{p}_{T}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle:=\left\{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}(\mathcal{X})|\mdmathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|u_{s}\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{p}\mathrm{d}s<\infty\right\},

\displaystyle\mdmathbb{H}^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2}))

\displaystyle:=\{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}(L^{\infty}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2}))|\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leqslant T\}}u_{t}\in L^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mdmathbb{P}\otimes\mathrm{d}x)\}.

If
T=\infty
, we may omit the subscript
T
in the spaces above.

•

We write
\rho\prec 1
if
\rho
is a smooth and compactly supported
function
\mdmathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow[0,1]
and analogously, we write
\rho\preceq 1
if
\rho\prec 1
or
\rho\equiv 1
. For a family of spatial
cut-offs
(\rho_{k})_{k}
will write
\rho_{k}\rightarrow 1
if
\rho\preceq 1
and

\lim_{k\to\infty}(1-\rho_{k}(x))=0
.

•

We reserve
\delta:=1-\beta^{2}/8\pi>0
to denote the
distance to criticality of the sine-Gordon model in our normalisation. The
relevant thresholds for us,
\beta^{2}<4\pi
,
\beta^{2}<6\pi
and

\beta^{2}<8\pi
, correspond to
\delta>\frac{1}{2}
,
\delta>\frac{1}{4}
and
\delta>0
respectively.

To study the Laplace transform of
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
, we will have to consider
localised perturbations
g+V
of the potential
V
for functionals
g:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
. This localisation will be
quantified in terms of the semi-norms

\displaystyle|g|_{1,p,k}:=\sup_{\varphi\in L^{p,k}}\|\nabla g(\varphi)\|_{L^{p,k}},

\displaystyle|g|_{2,p,k}:=\sup_{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in L^{p,k}}\frac{\|\nabla g(\phi_{1})-\nabla g(\phi_{2})\|_{L^{p,k}}}{\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{L^{p,k}}},

where we drop the parameter
k
if
k=0
. We always assume that
g,\nabla g
is
uniformly bounded, that is

\sup_{\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})}\|\nabla g(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}+|g(\varphi)|\leqslant L<\infty,

and that
g\in C^{2}_{b}(L^{2,-n})\cap C^{2}_{b}(H^{-\varepsilon,-n})
, the
space of functions
L^{2,-n}\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
with two continuous and
bounded derivatives with a continuous extension in
C^{2}_{b}(H^{-\varepsilon,-n})
.
The class of functions
g
satisfying the assumptions above is large enough to
be rate function determining (for a proof see e.g.
[
9
, Lemma
9]
).

2
Stochastic control set-up for Gibbs measures

In this section, we set up the general variational framework required to study
Gibbsian perturbations of the form (
1.4
) of a
Gaussian measure
\mu^{T}
from a stochastic control perspective. More
precisely, for a functional
g:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
satisfying the assumptions laid out in Section
1.2

and suitable functions
U\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mdmathbb{R})
and a spatial cut-off

\rho\prec 1
, we consider a generic perturbed potential

V^{g}(\varphi):=(g+V)(\varphi):=\lambda g(\varphi)+\lambda\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\rho(x)U(\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}x,

(2.1)

and study the generic Gibbs measures,

\nu(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=\nu^{V}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=\Xi_{V}^{-1}\exp(-V(\varphi))\mu^{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi).

(2.2)

We agree to drop the superscript
g
whenever
g=0
. Note that the measures

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T}
as defined in (
1.4
) are
precisely of this form whenever
\rho\prec 1,T<\infty
.

Before we can begin the analysis of the control problem, we have to construct
a suitable probability space. This requires a Brownian martingale
W
with the
Gaussian free field as its terminal value.

2.1
Scale decomposition

Mainly for technical convenience and concreteness, we use a heat kernel
decomposition to interpolate the covariance of the free field as

(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}Q_{t}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\quad\operatorname{with}\quad Q_{t}:=\left(\frac{1}{t^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-(m^{2}-\Delta)/t}\right)^{1/2}.

For a cylindrical Brownian motion
B
on
L^{2}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
, we then define
the Brownian martingale
(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
as the corresponding scale
interpolation of the Gaussian free field, that is

W_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}\mathrm{d}B_{s}.

By construction, the measure
\mu^{t}:=\operatorname{Law}(W_{t})
has covariance,

G_{t}(x,y):=G_{t}(x-y):=\int_{0}^{t}Q^{2}_{s}(x-y)\mathrm{d}s,

(2.3)

where we abuse the notation to use the same symbol for the operator and its
associated kernel on
L^{2}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
. A standard computation shows that
the kernels are explicitly given by,

\dot{G}_{s}^{1/2}(x)=Q_{s}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/2s}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{s}{2}|x|^{2}},\quad\dot{G}_{s}(x)=\frac{1}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/s}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{s}{4}|x|^{2}},\qquad x\in\mdmathbb{R}^{2}.

(2.4)

In addition to the smoothing property of the heat kernel, we rely on the following properties of the scale interpolation

•

the kernel of
\dot{G}
has a positive convolutional
square root
Q
, that is
\dot{G}_{t}=Q_{t}\ast Q_{t}
.

•

the kernels decay exponentially fast on
\mdmathbb R^{2}
, which is required to show the decay of correlations in Section

5
,

•

the kernels
\dot{G}_{s}
are invariant under Euclidean transformations, which is used in our proof of the Euclidean invariance in Section
8
.

Apart from the properties above, the precise choice of the scale
interpolation is not important for us and we will only require elementary
bounds on the kernels, all of which we collect in Appendix
A
.

A simple computation shows that the martingale
W
serves as a smooth
approximation to the free field. Before we proceed, let us note this fact for
future reference. We postpone the proof to Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For any
\varepsilon>0,p\in[1,\infty)
and

n>2
, the sequence
(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
converges in
L^{p}(\mathrm{d}\mdmathbb{P};B_{p,p}^{-\varepsilon,-n})
and almost surely to a random variable

W_{\infty}\sim\mu
, where
\mu
is the Gaussian free field, that is the
centred Gaussian measure on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
with covariance

(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}
. Moreover, for any
T<\infty
, the stopped process

(W_{t\wedge T})_{t\geqslant 0}
is a Gaussian process taking values in
the function space
L^{\infty,-n}
.

2.2
The control problem

With the scale interpolation
(W_{t})_{t}
of the free field, and thus the
probability space, constructed, we can return to the
measures (
2.2
). The goal of this section is to establish
the connection between Gibbsian perturbations of a Gaussian and the stochastic
control problem which is the basis for the FBSDE formulation.

Theorem 2.2
.

a)

For any
T\in[0,\infty)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})
, the FBSDE

Z^{g}_{t}(\varphi)=\varphi-\int_{0}^{t}\dot{G}_{s}\mdmathbb{E}_{s}[\nabla V^{g}(Z^{g}_{T}(\varphi)+W_{T})]\mathrm{d}s,

(2.5)

has a unique solution in
\mdmathbb{H}^{\infty}_{T}(L^{\infty})
.

b)

The process
X^{g}_{t}=Z_{t}^{g}+W_{t}
, where

Z_{t}^{g}
is the solution to (
2.5
), satisfies
\operatorname{Law}(X_{T}^{0})=\nu
and the pair

(\bar{u}^{g}_{t},X_{t}^{g})(\varphi):=(-Q_{t}\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\nabla V^{g}(X_{T}^{g}(\varphi))],X_{t}^{g}(\varphi)),

(2.6)

is the unique optimiser for the stochastic control problem,

X_{t}(u;\varphi)=Z_{t}(u;\varphi)+W_{t},\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{t}(u;\varphi):=\varphi+\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}u_{s}\mathrm{d}s

(2.7)

subject to the cost functional

\mathcal{V}^{V+g}(\varphi):=\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}J^{V^{g}}(u;\varphi):=\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}\mdmathbb{E}\left[V^{g}(Z_{T}(u;\varphi)+W_{T})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|u_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right].

(2.8)

In particular, the Laplace transform of
\nu
satisfies the variational
problem,

\mathcal{W}^{V}(g;\varphi):=-\log\nu_{\varphi}(\mathrm{e}^{-g})=\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}J^{V+g}(u;\varphi)-\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}J^{V}(u;\varphi).

(2.9)

Let us agree to drop the dependence on the initial value
\varphi
as long as
no ambiguities arise. This dependence on the initial value
\varphi
will only
become relevant in Section
8.3
and can safely be ignored for
the rest of the paper. We will arrive at Theorem
2.2
in
several steps. We start with the variational description for exponential
functionals of Brownian motion by Boué and Dupuis
(Lemma
2.3
). We then show that any optimally controlled process
has the correct law (Lemma
2.4
. Finally, we obtain necessary conditions on the optimal control
(Lemma
2.5
) and use a verification theorem to show existence and
uniqueness of an optimal control (Lemma
2.6
) which will
imply that the optimal dynamics is indeed given by (
2.5
).

We say a real valued random variable
Y
is
tame
(with respect to
the probability measure
\mdmathbb{P}
) if there are Hölder conjugates
p,q>1
(that is
1/p+1/q=1
) such that

\mdmathbb{E}[\exp(-qY)]+\mdmathbb{E}|Y|^{p}<\infty.

The assumptions on
g
and the
boundedness of
V
defined in (
2.1
) imply that this condition is
always satisfied for
Y=V^{g}(W_{t})
and
t\in[0,\infty)
. Recall the the
variational formula from
[
16
]
in
the more general version of
[
38
]
.

Theorem 2.3

(Boué–Dupuis)
.

Let
B
be a cylindrical Brownian motion on a Hilbert
space
H
and let
W=\int_{0}^{\cdot}Q_{t}\mathrm{d}B_{t}
be a Brownian motion
on
\tilde{H}
with covariance
G_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}^{2}\mathrm{d}s:H\rightarrow\tilde{H}
and define for
u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}
,

X_{t}(u)=Z_{t}(u)+W_{t},\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{t}(u)=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}sQ_{s}u_{s}.

(2.10)

For any Borel-measurable functional
F:\tilde{H}\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}

such that
F(W)
is tame, it holds that

-\log\mdmathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{-F(W)}]=\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}\mdmathbb{E}\left[F(X(u))+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|u_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right]=:\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}J^{F}(u).

(2.11)

Our interest in this formula is justified by the following observation. If
g:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
satisfies the
assumptions laid out in Section
1.2
, then
V^{g}
is tame and the
formula (
2.11
) provides a variational representation for the Laplace
transform of (
2.2
) via

\mathcal{W}^{V}(g)=-\log\nu(\mathrm{e}^{-g})=-\log\left(\frac{\mdmathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{-(g+V)(W_{T})}]}{\mdmathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{-V(W_{T})}]}\right)=\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}J^{V+g}(u)-\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}J^{V}(u).

(2.12)

If the infimum is a minimum, it turns out that the control problem actually
provides a more direct description of the measure
\nu
via the dynamics
X_{t}(u)
given by (
2.10
). We recall Lemma 11
from
[
6
]
, which is the key to
establish this relationship.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
g:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
be bounded and continuous. If for some
\alpha\in\mdmathbb{R}

the variational problem
\inf_{u\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}}J_{T}^{\alpha g}(u)
has a
minimiser
\bar{u}^{\alpha g}
, then
\alpha\mapsto\mathcal{W}^{V}(\alpha g)
satisfies

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\alpha}\mathcal{W}^{V}(\alpha g)=\mdmathbb{E}[g(X_{T}(\bar{u}^{\alpha g}))].

In particular,
\operatorname{Law}(X_{T}(\bar{u}))=\nu
.

Next, we show a necessary condition for the optimal control, which will also
provide a candidate for the minimiser of (
2.11
) as feedback control.

Lemma 2.5
.

If
\bar{u}^{g}\in\mdmathbb{H}_{a}
is optimal for the
control problem (
2.11
), then
\mathrm{d}t\otimes\mathrm{d}\mdmathbb{P}
-almost
surely,

\bar{u}_{t}^{g}=-Q_{t}\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\nabla V^{g}(X_{T}(\bar{u}^{g}))].

(2.13)

Proof.

Standard stability results for SDEs imply that the solution
X(u)
to
(
2.7
) is differentiable in
u
. Similarly, the regularity
assumed on
V
and
g
imply that also
J^{V+g}(u)
is differentiable
along all directions
\delta u\in\mdmathbb{H}^{2}_{T}(L^{2})
. We compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{\varepsilon}X_{t}^{u,\delta u}

\displaystyle:=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\right|_{\varepsilon=0}X_{t}(u+\varepsilon\delta u)=\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}\delta u_{s}\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\varepsilon}J^{u,\delta u}

\displaystyle:=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\varepsilon}\right|_{\varepsilon=0}J^{V+g}(u+\varepsilon\delta u)=\mdmathbb{E}\left[\nabla V^{g}(X_{T}(u))\nabla_{\varepsilon}X^{u,\delta u}_{T}+\int_{0}^{T}u_{s}\delta u_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right].

Since the control
u
has to be adapted, we may insert a conditional
expectation to find

\nabla_{\varepsilon}J^{u,\delta u}=\mdmathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}(Q_{s}\mdmathbb{E}_{s}[\nabla V^{g}(X_{T}(u))]+u_{s})\delta u_{s}\mathrm{d}s.

(2.14)

For an optimal control
u=\bar{u}^{g}
, it must hold for any direction

\delta u\in\mdmathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})
and
\varepsilon>0
,

J^{g}(\bar{u}^{g}+\varepsilon\delta u_{s})-J^{g}(\bar{u}^{g})\geqslant 0.

Moreover, since
\nabla Y_{t}^{g}(u):=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\nabla V^{g}(X_{T}(u))]
does not depend on the direction
\delta u
, we arrive at the claimed
first order condition for optimality

\bar{u}^{g}_{t}+Q_{t}\nabla Y^{g}_{t}(\bar{u}^{g})=0\Leftrightarrow\bar{u}^{g}_{t}=-Q_{t}\nabla Y_{t}^{g}(\bar{u}^{g}).

∎

Up until this point, we cannot guarantee existence of a minimiser. For the
potentials
V
as defined in (
2.1
), we can close this gap with a
verification theorem for feedback controls. Given a feedback control
u_{t}=\hat{u}_{t}(X_{t}(u))
, we say that the pair
(u,X(u))
is

admissible
if
X(u)
is a strong solution to the
SDE (
2.10
) controlled by
u
, that is
X(u)
is a strong solution
to the SDE

X_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}\hat{u}_{s}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s+W_{t},\quad t\in[0,T].

Lemma 2.6
.

The feedback control (
2.13
) is optimal
for the control problem (
2.8
). Moreover, the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}^{2}v_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}v_{t}\dot{G_{t}}\mathrm{D}v_{t},&t\in[0,T],\\
v_{T}=V+g,\end{cases}

(2.15)

has a unique bounded solution
v_{t}
and
\bar{u}^{g}
defined
in (
2.13
) satisfies

\bar{u}^{g}_{t}=-Q_{t}\nabla v^{g}_{t}(X_{t}(\bar{u}^{g})).

(2.16)

Proof.

Let us first show the statement for a bounded function
g
and write
v=v^{g}
,
V=V^{g}
. The Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation (HJB-equation; for
short) associated to the control problem (
2.8
) is given
by

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{t}+\inf_{a\in L^{2}}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}^{2}v_{t})+\langle\mathrm{D}v_{t},Q_{t}a\rangle_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\|a\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right\}=0,\\
v_{T}=V.\end{cases}

(2.17)

see e.g.
[
23
, Section 2.5.1]
. Solving
the quadratic optimisation problem in (
2.17
) we find that the
optimum is attained at
a=-Q_{t}\mathrm{D}v_{t}
so that the
PDE (
2.17
) reduces to (
2.15
). Define the function

v_{t}(\varphi):=-\log\mdmathbb{E}[\exp(-V(\varphi+W_{T}-W_{t}))].

(2.18)

Since
V
is bounded and smooth by assumption, the representation
in (
2.18
) implies that also
v
is smooth and bounded, say
v\in C^{1}_{b}([0,T],C^{2}_{b}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2}))
. We readily verify by a direct
computation that
v
is a solution to (
2.15
).

Having found a solution to the HJB equation (
2.17
), we have
access to the verification theorem (see
e.g.
[
23
, Theorem 2.36]
): if the
feedback control
\bar{u}
as defined in (
2.16
) is admissible
and satisfies for almost every
s\in[0,T]

\mdmathbb{P}
-almost surely,

\bar{u}_{s}\in\operatorname{argmin}_{a\in L^{2}}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}^{2}v_{s}(X_{s}(\bar{u})))+\langle\mathrm{D}v_{s}(X_{s}(\bar{u})),Q_{s}a\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\|a\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right\},

(2.19)

it follows that
\bar{u}
is optimal for the control problem. By the same
reasoning as before for the HJB-equation, the unique
L^{2}
-optimiser of
(
2.19
) is given by
\bar{u}_{s}=-Q_{s}\nabla v_{s}(X_{s}(\bar{u}))
. Since
V
is bounded, we see from (
2.18
) that the
solution
\exp(-v_{t}(\varphi))
is bounded away from
0
and the gradient
is given by

\nabla v_{t}(\varphi)=\frac{-\mdmathbb{E}[\nabla V(\varphi+W_{T}-W_{t})\exp(-V(\varphi+W_{T}-W_{t}))]}{\exp(-v_{t}(\varphi))}.

(2.20)

Hence, the gradient
\nabla v_{t}
inherits the Lipschitz continuity from
V

and
\nabla V
. As a result, the standard fixed point argument for SDEs with
bounded Lipschitz coefficients shows that the pair
(\bar{u},X(\bar{u}))

is admissible for the control problem. Finally, expanding the function
f_{s}:=\nabla v_{s}
along the flow of the optimally controlled process
X=X(\bar{u})
using Ito’s formula and the fact that
v_{s}

solves (
2.15
), yields

\displaystyle f_{t}(X_{t})

\displaystyle=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}\left[\nabla V(X_{T})-\int_{t}^{T}\left(\partial_{s}f_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}^{2}f_{s}-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}(f_{s}\dot{G}_{s}f_{s})\right)(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s\right]

(2.21)

\displaystyle=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\nabla V(X_{T})].

which is the missing equality

\bar{u}_{t}=-Q_{t}\nabla v_{t}(X_{t}(\bar{u}))=-Q_{t}\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\nabla V(X_{T}(\bar{u}))].

(2.22)

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

To see that (
2.5
) has a unique solution, note that by
(
2.22
), the SDE (
2.5
) is equivalent to
(
2.10
) with the feedback control
\bar{u}_{t}=-Q_{t}\nabla v_{t}(X_{t}(\bar{u}))
. By Lemma
2.6
this control is
admissible, i.e. there is a unique strong solution. By (
2.12
),
the variational problem for the Laplace transform is a direct consequence of
Lemma
2.3
. Lemma
2.5
and
2.6

imply combined that the pair defined in (
2.6
) is optimal
for the control problem. Moreover, the condition (
2.13
) is
necessary and since the solution to the SDE (
2.5
) is
unique, the pair
(\bar{u}^{g},X(\bar{u}^{g}))
defined
by (
2.6
) is the unique optimiser
for (
2.8
). Finally, Lemma
2.4
shows that the solution
X
to (
2.5
)
for
g=0
has the desired law,

\operatorname{Law}(X_{T})=\nu.

∎

Remark 2.7
.

a)

Compared to the more general setting considered
in
[
6
]
, the fact that the
potential is Lipschitz and bounded allows us to directly use the solution
to the HJB-equation (
2.15
) and enables the verification theorem.
This means that we do not need to relax the variational problem to ensure
existence of a minimiser. The difference is only a technical one and not
crucial to our analysis: the subsequent analysis could be carried out
verbatim for a relaxed version of the control problem, by possibly
enlarging the underlying filtration.

b)

We should emphasise the difference between
the two formulas

\bar{u}^{g}_{t}=-Q_{t}\nabla v^{g}_{t}(X_{t}(\bar{u}^{g})),

(2.23)

via the solution
v^{g}
to (
2.15
) and

\bar{u}^{g}_{t}=-Q_{t}\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\nabla V^{g}(X_{T}(\bar{u}^{g}))],

(2.24)

via the stochastic maximum principle. The PDE (
2.15
) is not only
non-linear but also infinite dimensional. The only reason we were able to
easily show well-posedness here are the explicit
formulas (
2.18
) and (
2.20
) for
v
and its
gradient. Both rely on the boundedness and Lipschitz continuity of
V^{g}

and its gradient. In our main application of interest, where
V=V^{\rho,T}
, both of these properties disappear as the regularisations
\rho
and

T
are removed. As a result, this strategy does not readily transfer to
the unregularised setting.

In contrast, the formula (
2.24
) yields the entirely
self-contained forward-backward dynamics (
2.5
). This
FBSDE is an appealing candidate for a stochastic quantisation equation for
the measures
\nu^{\rho,T}
that we can also transfer to the limit
\rho\rightarrow 1,T\rightarrow\infty
. Controlling (
2.5
)
uniformly in both regularisations is the objective of the next section.

2.3
The effective FBSDE

Motivated by the issues highlighted in Remark

2.7
-
b
, we move to a reformulation of
the FBSDE (
2.5
), which is stable in the
\rho\rightarrow 1
,

T\rightarrow\infty
limit and which can be studied without relying on a
direct analysis of the PDE (
2.15
). This means we do not have access to
the exact solution of (
2.15
). In place of the exact solution, we look
for a scale dependent function
(F_{t})_{t}
such that the error, or remainder,

R
defined by

R_{t}:=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[\mathrm{D}V(X_{T})]-F_{t}(X_{t}),

(2.25)

is small in a suitable sense. For
V=V^{\rho,T}
, we would like the
bounds to also be uniform in
\rho\prec 1
and
T<\infty
.
While we should keep this goal in mind, the ideas are more general and we
therefore first develop them for a generic function
\mathrm{D}V
. Similarly to the
computation in (
2.21
), we develop the function
F
along
the flow of the SDE (
2.5
) and obtain a BSDE for the remainder

R
,

\displaystyle R_{t}

\displaystyle=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}[F_{T}(X_{T})-F_{t}(X_{t})]

\displaystyle=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}H_{s}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s-\mdmathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{D}F_{s}(X_{s})\dot{G}_{s}R_{s}\mathrm{d}s+\mdmathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{D}F_{s}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}W_{s},

where

H_{t}(\varphi)=\left(\partial_{t}F_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}^{2}F_{t})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}(F_{t}\dot{G}_{t}F_{t})\right)(\varphi).

(2.26)

Since the stochastic integral is a martingale, it vanishes under the
conditional expectation. Allowing again a small perturbation
g
in the
potential, the optimal dynamics in (
2.5
) can equivalently be
described by the FBSDE

\begin{cases}X_{t}=\varphi+W_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\dot{G}_{s}(F_{s}(X_{s})+R_{s})\mathrm{d}s,\\
R_{t}=\mdmathbb{E}_{t}\left[\nabla g(X_{T})+\int_{t}^{T}H_{s}(X_{s})\mathrm{d}s-\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{D}F_{s}(X_{s})\dot{G}_{s}R_{s}\mathrm{d}s\right].\end{cases}

(2.27)

If there is no additional perturbation added to the potential
V
, choosing
F=\nabla v
for
v
the exact solution to (
2.15
), we
recover
R=0
. Even in this unperturbed case, the remainder proves to be useful as it allows us the freedom to
choose the function
F
, and let the remainder
R
compute the error resulting
from this approximation. We, therefore, set out to find a systematic way to
construct functions
F
for which the error term
H
is small in the next
section.

Remark 2.8
.

Observe that we really treat the function
g
in (
2.27
) as
a perturbation: we only develop the unperturbed gradient
\nabla V
along
the flow. The error due to
g
is collected entirely in the terminal
condition for the remainder
R
. This means that we only have to analyse the
flow equation for the unperturbed periodic potential
\nabla V
.
This distinction is of technical importance to us – in the subsequent analysis, we will use the periodicity of
\nabla V
and
V
to our advantage
and including a generic perturbation
g
would break this symmetry.

3
Analysis of the flow equation

In this section, we inductively derive the bounds on the coefficients of the
FBSDE (
2.27
) using a truncated version of the
renormalisation flow equation

\partial_{t}F_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}^{2}F_{t})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}(F_{t}\dot{G}_{t}F_{t})=0,\quad\text{subject to\quad}F_{T}=\mathrm{D}V^{T}.

(3.1)

3.1
Truncating the
flow

Heuristically, we expect that successive Picard iterations of the flow
equation (
3.1
) improve the the approximation. Accordingly, we
define an iterative scheme starting from
F^{[0]}:=0
and define

F^{[\ell]}
for
\ell>0
as the solution to the equation

\partial_{t}F^{[\ell]}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{t}\mathrm{D}^{2}F^{[\ell]}_{t})=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}(F_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}\dot{G}_{t}F^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}_{t}),

(3.2)

subject to the terminal conditions

F^{[\ell]}_{T}(\varphi)=\begin{cases}\nabla V^{T}(\varphi),&\text{for }\ell=1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(3.3)

for a suitable potential
V^{T}
to be determined later. The initial condition

F^{[0]}\equiv 0
ensures that (
3.2
) is triangular in
\ell

and we can solve (
3.2
) as a linear PDE with a source term.
Proceeding in this way, we define the
\ell^{\ast}
-th order approximation

F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}_{s}:=\sum_{\ell\leqslant\ell^{\ast}}F^{[\ell]}_{s}
. With this choice for
F
in the FBSDE (
2.27
),
the generator of the backward equation as defined in (
2.26
) reduces
to

\displaystyle H^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}_{s}

\displaystyle:=\partial_{s}F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}^{2}F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}_{s})-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{D}(F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}_{s}\dot{G}_{s}F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}_{s})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}>\ell^{\ast}\\
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\ell^{\ast}\end{array}$}}}\mathrm{D}(F_{s}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}\dot{G}_{s}F^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}_{s}).

The estimates on the flow equation will rely on the following simple Lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\lambda_{t}=\lambda\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}G_{t}(0)}
,
\bar{\lambda}_{t}:=|\lambda_{t}|
and
\delta=1-\frac{\beta^{2}}{8\pi}>0
. Then, for any
n\in\mdmathbb{N}
and
\alpha>1-n\delta
,

\int_{t}^{\infty}\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{n}\langle s\rangle^{-n}\langle s\rangle^{-\alpha}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim_{n}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{n}\langle t\rangle^{-(n-1)-\alpha}.

(3.4)

In particular, for
n\delta>1
we can choose
\alpha=0
and

\int_{t}^{\infty}\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{n}\langle s\rangle^{-n}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim_{n}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{n}\langle t\rangle^{-(n-1)}.

(3.5)

Proof.

With the heat kernel estimate (
A.1
) from
Lemma
A.1
, we see that
\bar{\lambda}_{t}\asymp C\bar{\lambda}(t\vee 1)^{1-\delta}
for some
C>0
. Now the claim follows from
\delta>0\Leftrightarrow\beta^{2}<8\pi
.
∎

Let us take a moment to heuristically explain how successive iterations
of (
3.2
) should improve in
\ell
. Starting from the first
order approximation
\ell=1
, the bilinear term does not give any
contributions, and the linear equation (
3.2
) computes the usual
Wick-ordering. In the specific case of the cosine interaction, this means more
concretely that

F^{[1]}_{t}(\varphi)=-\lambda_{t}\beta\sin(\beta\varphi),\qquad\text{where\quad}\bar{\lambda}_{t}:=\bar{\lambda}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}G_{t}(0)}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}C\langle t\rangle^{\beta^{2}/8\pi}=\bar{\lambda}C\langle t\rangle^{1-\delta}.

(3.6)

Here, we absorbed the coupling constant
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
into the
renormalisation constant
\lambda_{t}
. The estimates on
\lambda_{t}
are a
direct consequence of basic heat kernel estimates
(Lemma
A.1
). We directly read off the bounds,

\|\mathrm{D}F_{t}^{[1]}(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|F_{t}^{[1]}(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}\langle t\rangle^{1-\delta}.

(3.7)

Due to the form of the non-linearity of the flow equation (
3.2
)
and Lemma
3.1
, we can expect the bound

\|\mathrm{D}F^{[\ell]}_{t}(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|F^{[\ell]}_{t}(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell}\langle t\rangle^{-(\ell-1)}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}^{\ell}\langle t\rangle^{1-\ell\delta},

(3.8)

to propagate inductively. Indeed, assuming that the
bound (
3.8
) holds for all
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}<\ell
, we
obtain from Young’s inequality and the estimate
\|\dot{G}_{s}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\langle s\rangle^{-2}
that,

\|\mathrm{D}(F_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}\dot{G}_{t}F^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}_{t})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\|\mathrm{D}F_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\dot{G_{t}}\|_{L^{1}}\|F_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}}\langle t\rangle^{-(\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime})}.

(3.9)

Since
\dot{G}
is positive, formally integrating out the linear part in
(
3.2
) and passing to the mild formulation (see the next section
for details), this suggests

\|F^{[\ell]}_{t}(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\int_{t}^{T}\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{\ell}\langle s\rangle^{-(\ell-2)}\langle s\rangle^{-2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\int_{t}^{T}\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{\ell}\langle s\rangle^{-\ell}\mathrm{d}s.

Hence, Lemma
3.1
propagates the
bound (
3.8
) only if
\ell\delta>1
. Otherwise, we will
have to improve our analysis and introduce additional regularisations to
propagate the bounds from one level to the next. We therefore refer to the
terms with
\ell>1/\delta
as
irrelevant
and
\ell\leqslant 1/\delta
as
relevant
. To obtain uniform bounds on the remainder

R
in (
2.27
), the source term in (
2.27
)

H
should contain only irrelevant terms. The estimates (
3.9
)
suggest that

\|H_{t}^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}>\ell^{\ast}\\
\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\ell^{\ast}\end{array}$}}}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}}\langle t\rangle^{-(\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime})}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell^{\ast}}\langle t\rangle^{-\ell^{\ast}},

(3.10)

which is integrable in
t
from
\infty
for
\ell^{\ast}>1/\delta
by
Lemma
3.1
. The number of relevant terms depends on
the parameter
\beta^{2}
. If

\beta^{2}<\beta_{\ell^{\ast}}^{2}:=\left(\frac{\ell^{\ast}}{\ell^{\ast}+1}\right)8\pi,

(3.11)

then only terms at the levels
\ell\leqslant\ell^{\ast}
are relevant. At

\beta^{2}=8\pi
, the number of relevant terms is infinite and the model
reaches criticality. In the subcritical regime,
\beta^{2}<8\pi
, we see that
the number of relevant terms is finite, but grows arbitrary large as we
approach the critical value
\beta^{2}=8\pi
.

Indeed, for the first region,
\beta^{2}<\beta_{1}^{2}=4\pi
, only the first
level
\ell=1
is relevant and we can gather all higher order terms in the
remainder. Outside the first region, we have to deal with two related issues:

a)

due to (
3.10
), the terms
\ell<\ell^{\ast}
cannot be
included in the equation for
R
, so that we have to
iterate (
3.2
) at least up to
\ell^{\ast}
;

b)

the heuristic considerations suggest that the
bound (
3.8
) cannot naively propagate through the flow
equation on its own and these terms require renormalisation.

The goal of our subsequent analysis is to deal with both difficulties and
recover estimates to replace (
3.8
)
and (
3.10
) beyond this first threshold
\beta^{2}<4\pi
.

Since our analysis of the FBSDE is limited to the regime
\beta^{2}<\beta_{3}^{2}=6\pi
, we develop the ideas for the flow equation only up to this
threshold, where
\ell^{\ast}=3
is sufficient. We still emphasise that the
inductive reasoning produces (possibly field dependent) bounds on the
truncated flow in the entire subcritical regime
\beta^{2}<8\pi
.

3.2
The Fourier representation

To proceed with the iteration defined in (
3.2
) and finally
obtain estimates on
F_{s}^{[\ell]}
, we restrict our attention to a suitable
parametrised space of functions
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mdmathbb{R}^{d})
. Here, we use the periodicity of the potential to
our advantage and pass to a Fourier representation
following
[
18
]
. For a
\frac{2\pi}{\beta}
-periodic functional
V:\mdmathbb{R}_{+}\times\mdmathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mdmathbb{R}
we introduce the formal power series

V_{t}(\varphi)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}V_{t}^{[\ell]}(\varphi),

(3.12)

where with
\xi=(\sigma,x)\in\{-1,1\}\times\mdmathbb{R}^{2}
and

\xi_{1:\ell}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell})
, we define

V_{t}^{[\ell]}(\varphi):=\sum_{\sigma_{i}\in\{-1,1\}^{\ell}}\int_{(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}x_{1:\ell}f_{t}^{[\ell]}(\xi_{1:\ell})\mathrm{e}^{i\beta\sigma_{1}\varphi(x_{1})}\ldots\mathrm{e}^{i\beta\sigma_{\ell}\varphi(x_{\ell})}.

(3.13)

Since the level
\ell
is determined uniquely by the number of arguments

\xi_{1:\ell}
, we may drop the superscript
\ell
in
f^{[\ell]}
without
introducing ambiguities. For brevity of the subsequent notation, we write

[\ell]:=\{1,\dots,\ell\}
and introduce
the following shorthand for the integrals and the exponential fields,

\int\mathrm{d}\xi f(\xi):=\sum_{\sigma=\pm 1}\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}xf(\sigma,x),\quad{}_{x}^{\sigma}:=\mathrm{e}^{i\beta\sigma\varphi(x)},\quad\psi(\xi_{1:\ell}):=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}{}_{x_{i}}^{\sigma_{i}},

and for a set
I\subset[\ell]
we write
\xi_{I}=(\xi_{i})_{i\in I}
.
Finally, define the covariance matrix

\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell}):=-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}(G_{s}-G_{t})(x_{i}-x_{j}),\quad t\leqslant s.

(3.14)

With this notation and basic set-up, we can rewrite the flow
equation (
3.2
) in terms of the coefficients
f
. Since any
additive shift of the potential
V_{T}
by a constant does not affect the force,
the terminal condition (
3.3
) translates to

f_{T}^{[\ell],T}(\xi_{1:\ell})=\begin{cases}\frac{\lambda_{T}}{2},&\ell=1,\\
0,&\ell>1.\end{cases}

(3.15)

The functional
V^{[\ell]}
satisfies
the truncated flow equation (
3.17
) below at level
\ell
if and only
if, modulo positive combinatorial coefficients which we gather in
C_{|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|}
,

f_{t}^{[\ell],T}(\xi_{1:\ell})=-\beta^{2}\sum_{I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[\ell]}C_{|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})}f_{s}(\xi_{I_{1}})\Big[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I_{1}}\\
{j\in I_{2}}\end{subarray}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\dot{G}_{s}(x_{i}-x_{j})\Big]f_{s}(\xi_{I_{2}}).

(3.16)

We include a proof of the relevant implication in Lemma

3.3
at the end of this section.
Instead of controlling the functions
F
and
V
directly, we now want to
inductively derive estimates on these kernels
f^{[\ell]}
. Of course,
eventually we will be able to transfer these estimates back to
F
and
V
in
a straightforward manner (see Section
3.4
).

Before we proceed and derive bounds on the kernels
f
, some remarks about the
setup seem appropriate.

Remark 3.2
.

a)

We are primarily interested in the flow equation for the force.
However, for the variational description in Section
7
, we
will have to work at the level of the potential as well. Since the
equations for the force
F
are readily obtained by differentiating the
equations for
V
, we prefer to use it as a starting point. Up to an
additive constant, both descriptions are equivalent on the finite volume
and
F^{[\ell]}=\mathrm{D}V^{[\ell]}
satisfies (
3.2
) if and
only if
V^{[\ell]}
satisfies the Picard scheme for (
2.15
), that
is

\partial_{s}V^{[\ell]}_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}^{2}V^{[\ell]}_{s})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell}(\mathrm{D}V_{s}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}V^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}_{s}).

(3.17)

We nonetheless emphasise that we never rely on the fact that
F
is the
gradient of a potential in our analysis.

b)

The coefficients
f^{[\ell]}
are symmetric in their arguments

\xi_{1:\ell}
, i.e. for any permutation
\pi
of
[\ell]
,

f^{[\ell]}_{t}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\ell})=f^{[\ell]}_{t}(\xi_{\pi(1)},\ldots,\xi_{\pi(\ell)}).

(3.18)

c)

If
f^{[1]}
is translation
(respectively rotation) invariant, we inductively see
from (
3.16
) and the Euclidean invariance of the heat
kernel
\dot{G}
that also the kernels
f^{[\ell]}
at the higher levels

\ell>1
are translation (respectively rotation) invariant.
Correspondingly, if
f^{[1]}
is invariant under complex conjugation (that
is with
\bar{\xi}=(-\sigma,x)
we have
f^{[1]}(\xi)=f^{[1]}(\bar{\xi})
) then also
f^{[\ell]}(\xi_{1:\ell})=f^{[\ell]}(\bar{\xi}_{1:\ell})
is true for any
\ell>1
.

d)

We always consider truncations

V_{t}^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\ell^{\ast}}V_{t}^{[\ell]},\quad\text{and\quad}F_{t}^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\ell^{\ast}}F_{t}^{[\ell]},

of (
3.12
) for some
\ell^{\ast}<\infty
. Therefore,
we are not concerned with questions of convergence as
\ell^{\ast}\rightarrow\infty
. We will refer to the
truncated series
F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}_{s}
as the

\ell^{\ast}
-th order approximation, even though we do not
provide quantitative estimates on the convergence of the series

\sum_{\ell}F^{[\ell]}_{s}(\varphi)
. This can at least be motivated by
the observation that (
3.12
) is a formal power series in
the coupling constant
\lambda
, which formally solves the PDE (
3.17
). The fact that the representation is not
unique (both with respect to the summands in (
3.12
)
and the coefficients in (
3.13
)) does not cause any
inconvenience for us.

e)

The representation (
3.12
) is also known as Mayer
expansion in the literature and its convergence was already studied
in
[
18
]
and more recently in
a series of
papers
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
30
]

for the sine-Gordon model. In contrast to our analysis, these results
construct the exact solution to the flow equation (
3.17
) in different parameter regimes
\beta,\lambda
by showing that the formal
series (
3.12
) converges.

Lemma 3.3
.

If
(f^{[\ell]})_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}t\in[0,T]\end{array}$}}}
for
\ell\leqslant\ell^{\ast}
satisfy
(
3.16
), then
V^{[\ell]}_{t}
as defined in
(
3.13
) satisfies (
3.17
) for
\ell\leqslant\ell^{\ast}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f_{t}^{[\ell]}
satisfies (
3.16
) and define

V^{[\ell]}_{t}
according to (
3.13
). We compute,

\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}(\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}^{2}V^{[\ell]}_{s}(\varphi))=-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j}\int\mathrm{d}x_{i}\int\mathrm{d}x_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\dot{G}_{s}(x_{i}-x_{j})f_{t}^{[\ell]}(\xi_{1:\ell})\psi(\xi_{1:\ell}),

and with
|I_{1}|=\ell^{\prime}
,
|I_{1}|=\ell^{\prime\prime}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}V_{s}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}(\varphi)\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}V_{s}^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\int\mathrm{d}x_{I_{1}}f_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}(\xi_{I^{\prime}})\sum_{j\in I_{2}}\int\mathrm{d}x_{I_{2}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\dot{G}_{s}(x_{i}-x_{j})f_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}(\xi_{I_{2}})\psi(\xi_{I_{1}})\psi(\xi_{I_{2}}).

Differentiating the equation for
f^{[\ell]}
(
3.16
) we see that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}

\displaystyle f_{t}^{[\ell]}(\xi_{1:\ell})-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j\in[\ell]}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\dot{G}_{s}(x_{i}-x_{j})f_{t}^{[\ell]}(\xi_{1:\ell})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}I_{1}\dot{\cup}I_{2}=[\ell]\\
\end{array}$}}}C(|I_{1}|,|I_{2}|)f_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime}]}(\xi_{I_{1}})\left[\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\sum_{j\in I_{2}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\dot{G}_{s}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right]f_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}(\xi_{I_{2}}).

Inserting this into

\partial_{t}V_{t}^{[\ell]}(\varphi)=\sum_{\sigma_{i}\in\{-1,1\}^{\ell}}\int_{(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}x_{1:\ell}\partial_{t}f_{t}^{[\ell]}(\xi_{1:\ell})\mathrm{e}^{i\beta\sigma_{1}\varphi(x_{1})}\ldots\mathrm{e}^{i\beta\sigma_{\ell}\varphi(x_{\ell})},

yields the claim.
∎

3.3
Estimates on the Fourier
coefficients

In this section, we derive our main estimates on the kernels
f
defined
in (
3.16
) to control the flow under the conditional
expectation in (
2.5
). For
\varsigma\in(0,1)
and some
kernel
\kappa
to be chosen later (see (
3.25
) below), we will be using
the norms

\interleave f\interleave_{t}:=\sup_{\xi_{1}}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{2:\ell}|f(\xi_{1:\ell})\kappa_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell})\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1:\ell})|\quad\text{where }\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1:\ell}):=\mathrm{e}^{\varsigma m(\operatorname{St}(x_{1:\ell}))},

(3.19)

for the Fourier kernels (see (
1.13
) for the definition of the
Steiner diameter
\operatorname{St}(x_{I})
). If
\kappa_{t}\equiv 1
does not depend on
t
,
we may drop the subscript
t
. Since the coefficients
f
are symmetric in
their arguments (see also (
3.18
)), the point
\xi_{1}
is not special in any way
and the supremum could have been taken over any other
\xi_{k}
instead. The
exponential tree weights
\omega_{\varsigma}
allow us to quantify the decay
of the coefficients at large separation between the points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell}
, which we require to show decay of correlations in Section

5
. As

\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{I_{1}\cup I_{2}})\leqslant\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{I_{1}})\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{I_{2}})\mathrm{e}^{\varsigma md(x_{I_{1}},x_{I_{2}})},\quad\text{where\quad}d(x_{I_{1}},x_{I_{2}}):=\min_{x_{i}\in x_{I_{i}}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|,

these norms work nicely with the flow equation for the
coefficients (
3.16
) provided we choose
\varsigma\in(0,1)
. Indeed, since the convolution
\dot{G}_{s}
in (
3.16
)
always contracts along
(x_{i}-x_{j})
for
i\in I_{1}
and
j\in I_{2}
, Young’s
convolution inequality implies that for
\kappa_{t}\equiv 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{\xi_{1}}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{2:\ell}\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{I_{1}\cup I_{2}})\left|f^{[\ell^{\prime}]}_{t}(\xi_{I_{1}})\left[\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\sum_{j\in I_{2}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\dot{G}_{t}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right]f^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}_{t}(\xi_{I_{2}})\right|\\
\lesssim&\interleave f_{t}^{[\ell]}\interleave\interleave f_{t}^{[\ell^{\prime\prime}]}\interleave\|\dot{G}_{t}\|_{L^{1}(w_{\varsigma})}.\end{split}

(3.20)

We now want to make the heuristic bounds (
3.8
) precise and for an appropriate choice
for
\kappa_{t}
, we will show that for any
\ell\leqslant\ell^{\ast}
,

\interleave f_{t}^{[\ell]}\interleave_{t}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell}\langle t\rangle^{-(\ell-1)}.

(3.21)

To motivate our set-up going forward, consider
again (
3.16
). Because
\dot{G}_{s}
is a positive definite
kernel, it follows immediately from the definition (
3.14
) of
\Gamma
,

\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})\leqslant 0,\quad\text{for }t\leqslant s,

(3.22)

and consequently
\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})}\leqslant 1
. Applying
this estimate in (
3.16
) for
\ell=2
yields, with the
convolution inequality (
3.20
), the estimates on the first
order term in (
3.6
) and the heat kernel estimates from Lemma

A.4
using the assumption
\varsigma<1
,

\interleave f^{[2]}_{t}\interleave\lesssim\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\interleave f_{s}^{[1]}\interleave\interleave f_{s}^{[1]}\interleave\|\dot{G}_{s}\|_{L^{1}(\omega_{\varsigma})}\lesssim\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{2}\langle s\rangle^{-2},

which is not integrable from
\infty
unless
2\delta>1
(
\Leftrightarrow\beta^{2}<4\pi
). Therefore, we need additional help to propagate uniform
bounds along the flow. This help will partially come from the structure of the
covariance matrix
\Gamma_{t,s}
, and partially from the choice of
\kappa_{t}
in the
definition of the norm (
3.19
). To this end, define

q(\xi_{1:\ell}):=\sum^{\ell}_{k=1}\sigma_{k},

the
charge of
\xi_{1:\ell}
. We will call a contribution
\xi_{1:\ell}

neutral
if
q(\xi_{1:\ell})=0
and
charged

otherwise. The relevance of the charge is best illustrated by the improved
estimates on the covariance matrix
\Gamma_{t,s}
. If
\xi_{1:\ell}
is
charged, the exponential factor in (
3.16
) can help bring
down the scale. As a pleasant side effect, these estimates will also imply
that including an additional odd level, that is going from
\ell=2k
to

\ell+1
, introduces no new difficulties to the analysis. To not
interrupt the flow of ideas, we postpone the mostly technical proof to
Appendix
A.3
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose that
\xi_{1:\ell}
is charged. Then
there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
s\geqslant t
,

\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})\leqslant\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}(G_{t}(0)-G_{s}(0))+C,

(3.23)

and in particular

\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{-1}.

Remark 3.5
.

For neutral contributions,
q(\xi_{1:\ell})=0
, the
point-wise bound
\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})}\leqslant 1
is sharp:
If
x_{i}=0
for all
i=1,\ldots,\ell
, then we have
\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})=0
. As a result, point-wise estimates on the linear propagator

\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})}
cannot help to transport estimates for
the kernels
f
along the flow of (
3.16
). Conversely, if

|q(\xi_{1:\ell})|>1
, then it follows from the proof of Lemma

3.4
(see (
A.10
)) that we could
iterate the same procedure until only the neutral part remains and extract
more terms from the diagonal. In other words, the tighter bound

\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:\ell})\leqslant\frac{\beta^{2}}{8\pi}|q(\xi_{1:\ell})|(\log(t\vee 1)-\log(s\vee 1))+C,

is also true. For our purposes, the bound (
3.23
) will
always be sufficient.

With Lemma
3.4
, the integrability estimates from
Lemma
3.1
for
\alpha=1
show that the charged
contributions no longer pose a problem for us, allowing to set
\kappa_{t}\equiv 1

in this case. However, for the neutral contributions, this norm is too strong
and we will have to rely on the kernel
\kappa_{t}
.

Recall that
\dot{G}
is exponentially concentrated on
|x|\lesssim t^{-1/2}
, so that (see Lemma
A.4
),

\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x|x|^{2\alpha}\dot{G_{t}}(x)w_{\varsigma}(x)\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-2-\alpha}.

(3.24)

Combined with Lemma
3.1
, we expect that introducing
an additional zero of order
2\alpha
in
x_{i}-x_{j}
whenever
\sigma_{i}=-\sigma_{j}
should help to propagate a bound on a regularised version of the
kernel
f^{[\ell]}
. Of course, this regularisation comes at a price we have
to pay later. For now, let us ignore this issue and discuss how we can define
a regularised version of the kernels which allow to propagate the bounds for
the neutral contributions. With
\delta_{ij}x=x_{1}-x_{2}
,
c\in\left(0,\frac{1}{4}\right)
and
\alpha\in[0,1)
to be chosen later, we introduce
the (rotation and translation invariant) kernels

\kappa_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell}):=\begin{cases}t^{\alpha}|\delta_{12}x|^{2\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{ct|\delta_{12}x|^{2}},&\ell=2\text{ and }q(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=0,\\
1,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.25)

The increment
|\delta_{12}x|^{2\alpha}
ensures the integrability from

\infty
thanks to (
3.24
), the additional exponential weight in
the kernel is included for technical reasons that will become clear later and
the factor
t^{\alpha}
is included for convenience. Given a charge
q\in\mdmathbb{Z}
, we will also use the notation

f^{[\ell](q)}_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell}):=\mathbbm{1}_{\{q(\xi_{1:\ell})=q\}}f^{[\ell]}_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell}),

(3.26)

with analogous notations for the potential

V^{[\ell](q)}(\varphi):=\sum_{\sigma_{i}\in\{-1,1\}^{\ell}}\int_{(\mdmathbb{R}^{2})^{\ell}}\mathrm{d}x_{1:\ell}f_{t}^{[\ell](q)}(\xi_{1:\ell})\mathrm{e}^{i\beta\sigma_{1}\varphi(x_{1})}\ldots\mathrm{e}^{i\beta\sigma_{\ell}\varphi(x_{\ell})},

(3.27)

and the force
F^{[\ell],(q)}=\mathrm{D}V^{[\ell],(q)}
to consider the
bounds for charged and neutral contributions separately.

We can now proceed with the estimates on the regularised kernels for the
2
-point contributions, whose analysis already contains all additional
difficulties resulting from neutral contributions.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
\delta>0
and
\alpha>(1-2\delta)\vee 0
,

\interleave f_{t}^{[2]}\interleave_{t}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-1}.

(3.28)

Moreover, the kernels
f^{[2]}_{t}
inherit the concentration to
|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leqslant\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}
from
\dot{G}
. More precisely,
letting

\tilde{f}_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=f_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})|\delta_{12}x|^{2\kappa},

for some
\kappa\geqslant 0
, it holds that

\interleave\tilde{f}_{t}\interleave_{t}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-\kappa}\interleave f_{t}\interleave_{t}.

(3.29)

Proof.

By definition (
3.16
),

f^{[2]}_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=C\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}f^{[1]}_{s}(\xi_{1})\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\dot{G}_{s}(x_{1}-x_{2})f^{[1]}_{s}(\xi_{2}),

where for
\ell=1
,

|f^{[1]}_{s}(\xi)|\lesssim\bar{\lambda}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\beta^{2}G_{s}(0)}=\bar{\lambda}_{s}=\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{\ell}\langle s\rangle^{-(\ell-1)}.

(3.30)

We only show the bound (
3.29
), as
(
3.28
) follows directly by letting
\kappa=0
. We deal with
the two cases, charged and neutral, separately. If
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
is
charged, we use Young’s inequality, Lemma
3.4
and
the basic estimate (
3.24
) for
\alpha=0
, to conclude for
any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\interleave\tilde{f}_{t}^{[2](\pm 2)}\interleave_{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{\xi_{1}}\bar{\lambda}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{-1}\interleave f_{s}^{[1]}\interleave_{t}^{2}\||x|^{\kappa}\dot{G}_{s}\|_{L^{1}(w_{\varsigma})}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\bar{\lambda}_{s}\langle s\rangle^{-2-\kappa}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell}\langle t\rangle^{-(\ell-1)-\kappa}.

If
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
is neutral, we have to be more careful. By the
definition (
3.14
) of
\Gamma_{t,s}
, we can absorb the renormalisation
constants
\bar{\lambda}_{s}=\bar{\lambda}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}G_{s}(0)}
coming
from
f^{[1]}_{s}
through,

\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})+\beta^{2}G_{s}(0)=\beta^{2}G_{t}(0)-\beta^{2}G_{t}(x_{1}-x_{2})+\beta^{2}G_{s}(x_{1}-x_{2}).

Instead of the worst-case scaling
\beta^{2}G_{s}(0)
, for which only
point-wise estimates are possible, this means we only have to deal with

\beta^{2}G_{s}(x_{1}-x_{2})
. Here, combining the averaging in space with the
regularisation from the kernels
\kappa
defined in (
3.25
) allows
us to estimate the integral uniformly. Indeed, using the above we obtain

\displaystyle\interleave\tilde{f}_{t}^{[2](0)}\interleave_{t}

\displaystyle=C\sup_{\xi_{1}}\left|\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\kappa_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\kappa}\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1},x_{2})\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}f^{[1]}_{s}(\xi_{1})f^{[1]}_{s}(\xi_{2})\dot{G}_{s}(x_{1}-x_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{x_{1}}\left|\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1},x_{2})\dot{G}_{s}(x_{1}-x_{2})|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2\alpha+\kappa}t^{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{ct|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})+\beta^{2}G_{s}(0)}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{\beta^{2}G_{t}(0)}t^{\alpha}\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x|x|^{2\alpha+\kappa}\mathrm{e}^{ct|x|^{2}+\varsigma m|x|}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}_{s}(x)\mathrm{e}^{\beta^{2}G_{s}(x)-\beta^{2}G_{t}(x)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{\beta^{2}G_{t}(0)}t^{\alpha}\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x|x|^{2\alpha+\kappa}\mathrm{e}^{ct|x|^{2}+\varsigma m|x|}(\mathrm{e}^{\beta^{2}(G_{\infty}-G_{t})(x)}-1),

where we used that
\dot{G_{s}}
has a positive kernel in the last
inequality to replace
G_{T}
by
G_{\infty}
. Choosing
\alpha>(1-2\delta)\vee 0
we have access to (
A.6
) to compute the
integral over
x
above and obtain

\interleave\tilde{f}^{[2](0)}_{t}\interleave_{t}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{2}t^{\alpha}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\langle s\rangle^{-2-\alpha-\kappa}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-1-\kappa}=\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell}\langle t\rangle^{-(\ell-1)-\kappa}.

∎

With the dipole under control, we can propagate bounds on the (charged) subsequent contributions essentially for free using Lemma
3.4
. With some additional work, we will show improved estimates that do not rely on the kernel
\kappa
in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

For
\alpha\leqslant 1/2
and
\delta>1/4
, it holds that

\interleave f^{[3]}_{t}\interleave\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{3}\langle t\rangle^{-2}.

Proof.

By the definition (
3.16
) of the coefficients the kernel

f^{[3]}
is given by a linear combination of functions of the form

\bar{f}_{t}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})=C\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{e}^{\Gamma_{t,s}(\xi_{1:3})}f^{[1]}_{s}(\xi_{1})f^{[2]}_{s}(\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\sigma_{1}[\sigma_{2}\dot{G_{s}}(x_{1}-x_{2})+\sigma_{3}\dot{G_{s}}(x_{1}-x_{3})],

obtained by considering all the permutations of the arguments
(\xi_{i})_{i=1,2,3}
. If the
2
-point contribution
(\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
is charged,
applying (
3.20
) immediately implies the bound on

f^{[3]}
,

\interleave\bar{f}_{t}\interleave\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{-1}\langle s\rangle^{-2}\interleave f_{s}^{[1]}\interleave\interleave f_{s}^{[2](\pm)}\interleave\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{3}\langle t\rangle^{-2}=\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\ell}\langle t\rangle^{-(\ell-1)}.

Otherwise, if
(\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
is neutral, we only have uniform bounds on

\kappa_{t}f_{t}^{[2]}
but not on
f^{[2]}_{t}
. Therefore, we insert
1=\kappa_{t}\kappa_{t}^{-1}
and absorb
\kappa_{t}^{-1}
with the convolution
\dot{G}_{t}
to obtain the
bounds on
\bar{f}_{t}
. Here, we compute with
Lemma
A.3
, using the assumption
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
, and

\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1:3})\leqslant\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{2},x_{3})\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1},x_{2}).

Thus, writing
\delta_{ij}x:=x_{i}-x_{j}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{\xi_{1}}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{2:3}\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1:3})\frac{|\dot{G_{s}}(x_{1}-x_{2})-\dot{G_{s}}(x_{1}-x_{3})|}{s^{\alpha}|\delta_{23}x|^{2\alpha}}\mathrm{e}^{-ct|\delta_{23}x|^{2}}f_{s}^{[1]}(\xi_{1})(\kappa_{s}f_{s}^{[2]})(\xi_{2,}\xi_{3})\\
\lesssim&\sup_{\xi_{1}}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{2:3}(|\delta_{12}x|+|\delta_{23}x|)s^{-1/2}\mathrm{e}^{-ct|\delta_{12}x|^{2}+\varsigma m|\delta_{12}x|-m^{2}/s}f_{s}^{[1]}(\xi_{1})(\omega_{\varsigma}\kappa_{s}f_{s}^{[2]})(\xi_{2,}\xi_{3})\\
\lesssim&\interleave f_{s}^{[1]}\interleave\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{2:3}\mathrm{e}^{-cs|\delta_{12}x|^{2}+\varsigma m|\delta_{12}x|-m^{2}/s}[|\delta_{12}x|+|\delta_{23}x|]s^{-1/2}(\omega_{\varsigma}\kappa_{s}f^{[2]}_{s})(\xi_{2},\xi_{3}).\end{split}

(3.31)

By Young’s convolution inequality, the last integral can be estimated as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sup_{\xi_{1}}\left|\int\mathrm{d}(\xi_{2},\xi_{3})(\omega_{\varsigma}\kappa_{s}f_{s}^{[2]})(\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\mathrm{e}^{-cs|\delta_{12}x|^{2}+\varsigma m|\delta_{12}x|-m^{2}/s}[|\delta_{12}x|+|\delta_{23}x|]\right|\\
\lesssim&\sup_{x_{1}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}x_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-cs|\delta_{12}x|^{2}+\varsigma m|\delta_{12}x|-m^{2}/s}|\delta_{12}x|\interleave f_{s}^{[2]}\interleave_{s}\\
&+\sup_{x_{1}\in\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\int\mathrm{d}x_{2}\mathrm{e}^{-cs|\delta_{12}x|^{2}+\varsigma m|\delta_{12}x|-m^{2}/s}\interleave f^{[2]}_{s}|\delta_{23}x|\interleave_{s}.\end{split}

Using the scaling properties of
\kappa_{s}f_{s}^{[2]}
from
Lemma
3.6
and evaluating the Gaussian integral with
Lemma
A.4
using
\varsigma<1
and choosing
c\in\left(0,\frac{1}{4}\right)
in (
3.25
) sufficiently close to
1/4
, we
arrive at the required claim

\interleave\bar{f}_{t}\interleave\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{2}\langle s\rangle^{-3}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{3}\langle t\rangle^{-2}.

∎

Remark 3.8
.

The proof above more generally shows that with

\alpha\leqslant 1/2
,
\delta>1/4
and
|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|=\ell>2
, the following bounds hold,

\sup_{\xi_{1}}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{2:\ell}\omega_{\varsigma}(x_{1:\ell})|f_{t}(\xi_{I_{1}})|\left|\sum_{i\in I_{1}}\sum_{j\in I_{2}}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\dot{G}_{t}(x_{i}-x_{j})\right||f_{t}(\xi_{I_{2}})|\lesssim(\bar{\lambda}_{t}\langle t\rangle^{-1})^{\ell}.

(3.32)

We will rely on this later to obtain estimates on
H
in section
3.5
.

3.4
The renormalised problem

Given a spatial cut-off
\rho\prec 1
and a UV cut-off
T<\infty
, let

V_{t}^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}],\rho,T}(\varphi)=\sum_{\ell\leqslant\ell^{\ast}}[V^{[\ell],\rho,T}_{t}(\varphi)-c^{[\ell],\rho,T}],

(3.33)

for some suitable renormalisation constants
c^{[\ell],\rho,T}
to be chosen
later. Here, we denote by
V^{[\ell],\rho,T}
the
\ell
-th order
contribution as defined via its Fourier expansion as in
(
3.13
) subject to the condition

V_{T}^{[1],\rho,T}(\varphi)=\int_{\mdmathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)\lambda_{T}\cos(\beta\varphi).

We use the analogous definition and notation for the force
F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}],\rho,T}_{t}=\operatorname{D}V_{t}^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}],\rho,T}
and the
remainder
H^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}],\rho,T}
defined in (
2.26
).
For
\beta^{2}<6\pi
and
\ell^{\ast}=3
, we transfer the bounds we
obtained for the Fourier coefficients
f^{[\ell]}
in the previous section to
the truncated potential
V^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}
and the truncated force

F^{[\leqslant\ell^{\ast}]}
. In this step, we have to pay the price for the
regularisation with the kernels
\kappa
defined in (
3.25
). For
\beta^{2}<6\pi
, these kernels only appear at level
\ell=2
, and by definition,
