\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\|\dot{G}_{t}(z)/|z|^{{}_{1}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathrm{d}z)}\|\chi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}}\bar{\lambda}_{t}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\varepsilon}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{-2}t^{{}_{3}}\mathbb{M}^{c}

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\langle t\rangle^{-2+{}_{1}/2+{}_{3}}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\varepsilon}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{-1}\mathbb{M}^{c}.

Integrating over the scales and using the usual estimate for

\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{t}
for
c\in(0,1)
we find

\begin{aligned} \int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\mathrm{d}t&\lesssim_{\mathbb{M}^{c}}&r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-3+{}_{1}/2+{}_{3}+\delta}\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{\varepsilon}\end{aligned}.

For
\delta=1/2
, we find for
{}_{3}>2-3\delta
and
{}_{1}>0
sufficiently small for some
\bar{\gamma}\in(0,1/2)
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-3+{}_{1}/2+{}_{3}+\delta}\langle t\rangle^{1-\delta}\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-3/2+\bar{\gamma}}<\infty,

so that for any
\gamma>0
,

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\mathrm{d}t\lesssim C(\mathbb{M}^{c})\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\log(\varepsilon^{-2})^{-\gamma}\varepsilon^{2\delta}<\infty,\quad a.s.

For
\delta<1/2
, choosing
{}_{1}>0
,
{}_{3}>2-3\delta

sufficiently small, we find for some
\bar{\gamma}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-3+{}_{1}/2+{}_{3}+\delta}\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-2}}\langle t\rangle^{-2+{}_{1}/2+{}_{3}}+r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\varepsilon^{-2(1-\delta)}\int_{\varepsilon^{-2}}^{\infty}\langle t\rangle^{-3+{}_{1}/2+{}_{3}+\delta}

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{2\delta}(1+\varepsilon^{-2+6\delta-\bar{\gamma}}),

so that the right-hand side is uniformly bounded provided
\gamma>2(1-4\delta)
.

(
\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
).
In the same way as for

\mathcal{C}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
, we rewrite
\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
in
terms of the trigonometric functions

\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}=r(\varepsilon)\int\mathrm{d}z\beta^{2}(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)\lambda_{t}\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}\int\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)\cos(\beta(\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x))),

In contrast to the charged contribution, the Wick ordering
(
6.4
) now introduces a divergent contribution instead.
Therefore, we split
\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}
once more as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}=

\displaystyle r(\varepsilon)\int\mathrm{d}z\beta^{2}(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)\lambda_{t}\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}\int\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)

\displaystyle+r(\varepsilon)\int\mathrm{d}z\beta^{2}(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)\lambda_{t}\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}\int\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)(\cos(\beta(\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x)))-1)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle c^{\varepsilon}_{t}+r(\varepsilon)\int\mathrm{d}z\beta^{2}(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)\lambda_{t}\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}\int\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)(\cos(\beta(\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x)))-1).

We claim that under the assumptions on
r(\varepsilon)
, the constant

c^{\varepsilon}_{t}
diverges, while the difference
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}|\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}-c_{t}^{\varepsilon}|
is almost surely finite.
Indeed, from the asymptotics of
G^{\varepsilon}
in Lemma
6.3

below, it follows that for any
c>0
, and a constant
C
allowed to change
from line to line,

\displaystyle c_{t}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=r(\varepsilon)\int\mathrm{d}z\beta^{2}(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)\lambda_{t}\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}=Cr(\varepsilon)\|\chi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}}\|\dot{G}_{t}\|_{L^{1}}(\lambda_{t}\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}+O(1))

\displaystyle=Cr(\varepsilon)\langle t\rangle^{-2}(\lambda_{t}\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}+O(1)),

where we used that
\|\chi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{1}}=\|\chi\|_{L^{1}}
. We
again split the integral over the scales at
\varepsilon^{-2}
to extract
the divergent contribution, using the bounds
\lambda_{t}^{\varepsilon}
from
Lemma
6.3
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-2}}c_{t}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\beta^{2}r(\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-2}}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-1-\delta}\langle t\rangle^{1-\delta}+O(1),

\displaystyle=\beta^{2}r(\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-2}}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-2\delta}+O(1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}C\log(\varepsilon^{-2})^{-\gamma}(\log(\varepsilon^{-2})+O(1)),&\delta=1/2,\\
Cr(\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon^{-2+4\delta}),&\delta<1/2,\end{cases}

while

\int_{\varepsilon^{-2}}^{\infty}c_{t}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}t=Cr(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{-2(1-\delta)}\int^{\infty}_{\varepsilon^{-2}}\langle t\rangle^{-1-\delta}\mathrm{d}t=Cr(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{-2+2\delta}=\begin{cases}Cr(\varepsilon),&\delta=1/2,\\
C\varepsilon^{\gamma-2+2\delta},&\delta<1/2.\end{cases}

Combined this implies with the assumptions
\gamma<1
, in case
\delta=1/2
and
\gamma<2(1-2\delta)
, that

\int_{0}^{\infty}c_{t}^{\varepsilon}\mathrm{d}t=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}C\log(\varepsilon^{-2})^{-\gamma}(\log(\varepsilon^{-2})+O(1)),&\delta=1/2\\
Cr(\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon^{-2+4\delta}),&\delta<1/2\end{array}\right\}\overset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\infty.

It remains to show that
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}-c_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\mathrm{d}t<\infty
almost
surely. To this end, we Taylor expand the cosine as

(\cos(\beta(\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x)))-1)=|\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x)|^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\cos(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x)))).

Recall that for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\sup_{t}\left\|\langle t\rangle^{-\alpha/2}{\varphi_{t}}\right\|_{B^{\alpha-}_{\infty,\infty}}<\infty
almost surely by Lemma
4.8
, so that for any

1

|\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{t}(x)|\lesssim\left(|z|^{2}\|\varphi_{t}\|_{B^{2}_{\infty,\infty}}+\varepsilon^{{}_{1}}\|\varphi_{t}\|_{B^{{}_{1}}_{\infty,\infty}}\right)\lesssim\mathbb{X}(|z|^{2}\langle t\rangle^{1+}+\varepsilon^{{}_{1}}\langle t\rangle^{{}_{1}/2+}),

where
\mathbb{X}:=\sup_{t}\|\langle t\rangle^{-{}_{1}/2-}\varphi_{t}\|_{B^{{}_{1}}_{\infty,\infty}}\vee\sup_{t}\|\langle t\rangle^{-1-}\varphi_{t}\|_{B^{2}_{\infty,\infty}}
is almost surely
finite.

Regarding the cosine term, we proceed similarly to the charged case,
repeatedly applying the trigonometric identities

\displaystyle\cos(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x))))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}(x))))\cos(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x))))

\displaystyle-\sin(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}(x))))\sin(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x)))).

As before, we restrict our attention to the cosine term, with the analysis for
the sines being analogous. The difference due to the mollification can be
repeated verbatim to obtain

\cos(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(x)-\varphi_{t}^{\varepsilon}(x))))\lesssim\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\|Z_{t}\|_{B^{{}_{2}}_{\infty,\infty}},

knowing that
\sup_{t}\|Z_{t}\|_{B^{{}_{2}}_{\infty,\infty}}<\infty

almost surely provided
{}_{2}<2\delta
. For the remaining term, we
again insert the Wick ordering and apply the trigonometric identities for

\varphi=Z+W
to obtain

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)\llbracket\cos(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}(x))))\rrbracket

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\llbracket\cos(\vartheta\beta(W_{t}(z-\cdot)-W_{t}(\cdot)))\rrbracket\rho(x)\|_{L^{1}}\|\cos(\vartheta\beta(Z_{t}(z-\cdot)-Z_{t}(\cdot)))\|_{L^{\infty}}.

It follows from Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
6.4

below that

\|\cos(\vartheta\beta(Z_{t}(z-\cdot)-Z_{t}(\cdot)))\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|Z_{t}(z-\cdot)-Z_{t}(\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{1/2-\delta}|z|.

Combined with Lemma
6.5
, for
\delta^{\prime}=1-(\beta\vartheta)^{2}/8\pi
,
{}_{3}>-1+4(1-\delta^{\prime})
and
{}_{4}>1/2

\int\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)\llbracket\cos(\vartheta\beta((\varphi_{t}(z-x)-\varphi_{t}(x))))\rrbracket\leqslant t^{{}_{4}+1/2-\delta}|z|^{-{}_{3}+1}\mathrm{e}^{-\vartheta\beta^{2}G_{t}(z)}\mathbb{M}^{n},

where

\mathbb{M}^{n}:=\sup_{t,z}t^{-{}_{4}}|z|^{{}_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{\vartheta\beta^{2}G_{t}(z)}\|\rho(\cdot)\llbracket\cos(\vartheta\beta(W_{t}(z-\cdot)-W_{t}(\cdot)))\rrbracket\|_{L^{1}}<\infty,\quad a.s.

Combined, for some implicit random but almost surely finite constant
depending on
\mathbb{X},\mathbb{M}^{n}
and
\|Z_{t}\|_{B^{{}_{2}}_{\infty,\infty}}
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}-c_{t}^{\varepsilon}|

(6.5)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\int\mathrm{d}z(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)\bar{\lambda}_{t}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\varepsilon}(|z|^{2}\langle t\rangle^{1+}+\varepsilon^{{}_{1}}\langle t\rangle^{{}_{1}/2+})t^{-{}_{4}+1/2-\delta}|z|^{-{}_{3}+1}\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta\vartheta)^{2}G_{t}(0)}.

Since
2-4(1-\delta)>-d
, we can choose
\tilde{\gamma}_{3}=-{}_{3}+1<2-4(1-\delta^{\prime})
sufficiently large so that

\tilde{\gamma}_{3}>-d
and

\varepsilon^{2}\int(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)|z|^{\tilde{\gamma}_{3}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2+\tilde{\gamma}_{3}}\langle t\rangle^{-2},

and in the same way

\int(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)|z|^{\tilde{\gamma}_{3}+2}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-2-\tilde{\gamma}_{3}/2-1}.

Inserting these bounds in (
6.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{N}^{\varepsilon}_{t}-c_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\lesssim

\displaystyle r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\int\mathrm{d}z(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)|z|^{-\tilde{\gamma}_{3}+2}\bar{\lambda}_{t}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\varepsilon}t^{{}_{4}+1/2-\delta+1}\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta\vartheta)^{2}G_{t}(0)}

\displaystyle+r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\varepsilon^{{}_{1}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\int\mathrm{d}z(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)|z|^{-\tilde{\gamma}_{3}}\bar{\lambda}_{t}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{\varepsilon}\langle t\rangle^{{}_{1}/2}t^{{}_{4}+1/2-\delta}\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta\vartheta)^{2}G_{t}(0)}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle(I^{a}_{t})+(I^{b}_{t}).

Integrating over the scales with the estimate on
\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{t}

from Lemma
6.3
, we obtain for the first term after using the
conditions on
i
,
i=1,\ldots,4
, we have for some
\bar{\gamma}>0
arbitrarily small,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-2}}(I^{a}_{t})\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-2}}\mathrm{d}tr(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\int\mathrm{d}z(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)|z|^{-\tilde{\gamma}_{3}+2}t^{2(1-\delta)}t^{{}_{4}+1/2-\delta+1}\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta\vartheta)^{2}G_{t}(0)}

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-2}}t^{-3\delta}t^{\bar{\gamma}/2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}(\varepsilon^{-2+6\delta-\bar{\gamma}}+1),

and

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon^{-2}}^{{}^{\infty}}(I^{a}_{t})\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\varepsilon^{-2(1-\delta)}\int^{\infty}_{\varepsilon^{-2}}\mathrm{d}t\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\int\mathrm{d}z(\chi^{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{t})(z)|z|^{-\tilde{\gamma}_{3}+2}t^{1-\delta}t^{{}_{4}+1/2-\delta+1}\mathrm{e}^{-(\beta\vartheta)^{2}G_{t}(0)}

\displaystyle\lesssim r(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\varepsilon^{-2+6\delta-\bar{\gamma}}.

Choosing
i
,
i=1,\ldots,4
such that
\bar{\gamma}<{}_{2}<2\delta
, we see that this term is uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
in
the case
\delta=1/2
. Similarly, choosing
\bar{\gamma}
sufficiently
small and
2
sufficiently large, the condition
\gamma>2(1-3\delta)>2(1-4\delta)
implies the boundedness for
\delta<1/2
.

For the second term, we argue similarly, again using the conditions on

i
we have for some arbitrarily small
\bar{\gamma}>0
,

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}(I_{t}^{b})\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}r(\varepsilon)(\varepsilon^{{}_{2}}\varepsilon^{-2+6\delta-\bar{\gamma}}+1)<\infty,

due to the assumptions on
\gamma
. ∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Using the notation introduced in (
6.3
), it
holds that

\begin{aligned} G_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log((\varepsilon^{-2}\wedge t)\vee 1)+O(1)\end{aligned}.

(6.6)

Proof.

By definition of
G^{\varepsilon}
, it holds that

G_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0)=\mathbb{E}[|W_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0)|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}|\langle W_{t},\chi_{\varepsilon}\rangle|^{2}=\langle\chi_{\varepsilon},G_{t}\chi_{\varepsilon}\rangle.

For
t>\varepsilon^{-2}
, passing to Fourier space this implies with

G_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s^{-2}}\mathrm{e}^{-(m^{2}-\Delta)/s}=\mathrm{e}^{-(m^{2}-\Delta)/t}(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1},

\langle\chi^{\varepsilon},G_{t}\chi^{\varepsilon}\rangle=\int\mathrm{d}\xi\frac{|\hat{\chi}^{\varepsilon}(\xi)|^{2}}{m^{2}+|\xi|^{2}}-\int\mathrm{d}\xi\frac{|\hat{\chi}^{\varepsilon}(\xi)|^{2}}{m^{2}+|\xi|^{2}}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-(m^{2}+|\xi|^{2})/t}).

The second term is uniformly bounded using
t<\varepsilon^{-2}
, and
moreover as
(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}(m^{2}+|\xi|^{2})})\rightarrow 0

as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, vanishes in the limit by dominated
convergence. For the first term, using
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\chi}\subset B_{1}(0)
, we find

\int\mathrm{d}\xi\frac{|\hat{\chi}^{\varepsilon}(\xi)|^{2}}{m^{2}+|\xi|^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\frac{r}{m^{2}+r^{2}}\mathrm{d}r=O(1)+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{1}^{\varepsilon^{-1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(\varepsilon^{-2}\vee 1)+O(1).

In the case
t<\varepsilon^{-2}
, we use

\int\mathrm{d}x(\chi^{\varepsilon}(x)-\delta(x))G_{t}(x)\lesssim\varepsilon^{\alpha}\|G_{t}\|_{B^{\alpha}_{\infty,\infty}}\lesssim t^{-\alpha/2}t^{\alpha/2}=O(1),

to obtain,

\displaystyle G_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}x\chi^{\varepsilon}(x)\int\mathrm{d}y\chi^{\varepsilon}(y)G_{t}(x-y)

\displaystyle=G_{t}(0)+\int\mathrm{d}x(\chi^{\varepsilon}(x)-\delta(x))G_{t}(x)+\int\mathrm{d}x\chi^{\varepsilon}(x)\int\mathrm{d}y(\chi^{\varepsilon}(y)-\delta(y))G_{t}(x-y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(t\vee 1)+O(1).

∎

Lemma 6.4
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
t\in[0,\infty)
,

\|Z_{t}(z-\cdot)-Z_{t}(\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim t^{1/2-\delta}|z|.

Proof.

This follows directly from (
A.4
) in
Lemma
A.1
, and the FBSDE (
4.1
) for

Z=X-W
.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

(N)
For any
{}_{1}>1/2
,

{}_{2}>-1+4(1-\delta)
it holds that

\sup_{t,z}t^{-{}_{1}}|z|^{{}_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\beta^{2}G_{t}(z)}\|\llbracket\cos(\beta(W_{t}(z-\cdot)-W_{t}(\cdot)))\rrbracket\rho(\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}<\infty,\quad a.s.

(C)
For any
{}_{1}>0
,
{}_{2}>2-3\delta
and
s<2\delta
sufficiently large, it holds that

\sup_{t,z}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\beta^{2}G_{t}(z)}|z|^{{}_{1}/2}t^{-{}_{2}}\llbracket\cos(\beta(W_{t}(\cdot-z)+W_{t}(\cdot)))\rrbracket\rho(\cdot)\|_{B^{-s}_{p,p}(\mathrm{d}x)}<\infty,\quad a.s.

The proof of this lemma is given on page
C
at the end
of Appendix
C
.

7
Variational description and large deviations

7.1
Finite volume

If
\beta^{2}<4\pi
, the variational description in the finite volume is
essentially a direct consequence of the convergence of the Wick-ordered cosine
and the refinement of the Boué–Dupuis formula (Lemma
2.3
)
from
[
38
]
. Beyond the first
threshold, the apparent singularity of the sine-Gordon measure means that both
the renormalised potential, and the quadratic part in the cost functional

J^{\rho,g}:=J^{V^{\rho}+g}
as defined in (
2.8
)
cannot be expected to stay bounded as
T\rightarrow\infty
. To overcome this
difficulty, we follow the same strategy as for the FBSDE and introduce a
change of variables that isolates the singular part of the control from a more
regular remainder. In these new variables, we can again recover uniform
estimates and pass to the limit for any coupling constant
\bar{\lambda}
.
Throughout this section, we assume that
\rho\prec 1
is fixed and suppress the
dependency on
\rho
and
g
whenever no ambiguities arise.

Translating the same ideas as before now to the level of the variational
problem, we begin by developing the potential along the flow. This yields by
Ito’s formula

V_{T}(X_{T}^{u})=V_{t}(X_{t}^{u})+\int_{t}^{T}\left[\left(\partial_{s}V_{s}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}^{2}V_{s}\right)(X_{s}^{u})+\mathrm{D}V_{s}(X^{u}_{s})Q_{s}u_{s}\right]\mathrm{d}s+\operatorname{mart.},

(7.1)

where we use the shorthand

X_{t}^{u}:=X_{t}(u)=\varphi+\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}u_{s}\mathrm{d}s+W_{t}.

Again, we want to use the fact that
V
approximately solves the flow
equation (
3.17
). Adding the missing terms we can insert the remainder

\mathcal{H}
as defined in (
3.46
) and rewrite (
7.1
)
as

V_{T}(X_{T}^{u})=V_{t}(X_{t}^{u})+\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\left\{\mathcal{H}_{s}(X_{s}^{u})+\mathrm{D}V_{s}(X^{u}_{s})Q_{s}u_{s}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{D}V_{s}\dot{G}_{s}\mathrm{D}V_{s})(X_{s}^{u})\right\}+\text{mart.}.

(7.2)

Since
\mathcal{H}
is integrable in the scale parameter
t
from
\infty
,
it remains to deal with the quadratic terms. Using the notation

z^{u}_{s}:=-Q_{s}F_{s}(X^{u}_{s}),

for the singular part of the control, the variational
problem (
2.8
) becomes upon inserting (
7.2
)
for
V_{T}
and completing the square,

\displaystyle\inf_{u\in\mathcal{A}}J_{T}^{g}(u)

(7.3)

\displaystyle=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathbb{E}\left[g(X_{T}^{u})+V_{0}(X_{0}^{u})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\left\{\mathcal{H}^{T}_{s}\left(X_{s}^{u}\right)-\langle z_{s}^{u},u_{s}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\|z^{u}_{s}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|u_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right\}\right],

\displaystyle=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathbb{E}\left[g(X_{T}^{u})+V_{0}(X_{0}^{u})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\left\{\mathcal{H}^{T}_{s}(X_{s}^{u})+\frac{1}{2}\|u_{s}-z^{u}_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right\}\right].

Importantly, this reformulation no longer imposes square integrability on the
control
u
but only on
u-z^{u}
, which heuristically corresponds to the
(more regular) remainder. We take this as an invitation to introduce the
change of variables

r_{t}^{u}:=u_{t}-z^{u}_{t}.

(7.4)

The following Lemma ensures that this change of variables does not affect the
variational problem (
7.3
).

Lemma 7.1
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})
,
T\leqslant\infty
and

\rho\prec 1
, there is a unique solution
\hat{Z}^{r}\in\mathbb{H}^{\infty}(L^{\infty})
to the SDE

\hat{Z}^{r}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}Q_{s}(r_{s}-Q_{s}F^{\rho,T}_{s}(\hat{Z}^{r}_{s}+W_{s}))\mathrm{d}s.

(7.5)

In particular, with
\hat{X}=\hat{Z}+W
, defining
u^{r}_{t}:=-Q_{t}F^{\rho,T}_{t}(\hat{X}^{r}_{t})+r_{t}
, the control
u^{r}
is admissible for the
finite-horizon control problem (
7.3
) and we have
\hat{X}^{r}\equiv X^{u^{r}}
and
r_{t}=u^{r}_{t}-z_{t}^{u^{r}}
almost surely.

Proof.

The estimates on the approximate solution to the flow equation imply that

Q_{t}F_{t}^{\rho,T}
is globally Lipschitz and bounded, uniformly in
t
. The
result now follows from standard well-posedness for SDEs with Lipschitz
coefficients.
∎

This allows yet another reformulation of (
7.3
) in terms of
the remainder. To avoid confusion with the infinite volume control problem
later, let us make the dependence on
\rho
explicit again and define the cost
functional

\hat{J}_{T}^{\rho,g}(r):=\mathbb{E}\left[g(\hat{X}_{T}^{r})+V^{\rho}_{0}(\hat{X}_{0}^{r})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\left\{\mathcal{H}^{\rho,T}_{s}(\hat{X}_{s}^{r})+\frac{1}{2}\|r_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right\}\right],

(7.6)

with
\hat{X}^{r}
defined as the unique solution to (
7.5
). Observe
that in contrast to
J^{\rho,g}_{T}
, the functional
\hat{J}^{\rho,g}_{T}

satisfies for some
C=C_{g}>0
,

\hat{J}^{\rho,g}_{T}(r)\geqslant-C+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\|r_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

(7.7)

which we immediately verify from (
7.6
) and the estimates on

\mathcal{H}
(see Proposition

3.13
-
b
). In particular, the
cost functional
\hat{J}_{T}^{\rho,g}
makes sense also at
T=\infty
.
From (
7.7
) we see that
\hat{J}^{\rho,g}_{T}(r)=\infty
, whenever
r\not\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{T}(L^{2})
. We can thus enlarge the set
over which we take the infimum and use Lemma
7.1
to see
for any
T<\infty
,

\inf_{r\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\hat{J}_{T}^{\rho,g}(r)=\inf_{r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})}\hat{J}_{T}^{\rho,g}(r)=\inf_{u\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}J^{\rho,g}_{T}(u).

Therefore, the relation between the FBSDE (
4.1
) and the variational
problem (
2.8
) obtained in Theorem

2.2
-
b
is also valid for the UV-limit,
provided we renormalise the form of the cost functional.

Theorem 7.2
.

Denote by
(Z^{\rho,g},R^{\rho,g})
the unique
solution to (
4.1
) with
\rho\prec 1
and
T=\infty
. Then,

r^{\rho,g}_{t}:=-Q_{t}R^{\rho,g}_{t},

(7.8)

is admissible and optimal for the control problem (
7.6
) at
T=\infty
.

Proof.

Let us fix a cut-off
\rho\prec 1
and leave the dependence implicit for this
proof. Thanks to Lemma
4.1
, the candidate for the optimal
control for the control problem defined in (
7.8
)
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|r^{g}_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s<\infty,

and thus
r^{g}_{s}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
so that

\inf_{r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})}\hat{J}_{\infty}^{g}(r)\leqslant\hat{J}_{\infty}^{g}(r^{g}).

It remains to show the reverse inequality. To this end, let
\bar{u}^{T,g}=-Q_{t}(R^{T,g}_{t}+F_{t}(X^{T,g}_{t}))
be the optimal control for the
control problem (
2.8
). Then, for any finite
T
,
(writing
\bar{z}^{T,g}\equiv z^{\bar{u}^{T,g}}
and
X^{T,g}=X^{u^{T,g}}=Z^{T,g}+W
), it holds that

\bar{r}_{t}^{T,g}:=\bar{u}^{T,g}_{t}-\bar{z}_{t}^{T,g}=-Q_{t}(R^{T,g}_{t}+F_{t}(X^{T,g}_{t}))+Q_{t}F_{t}(X_{t}^{T,g})=-Q_{t}R_{t}^{T,g},

is optimal for the control problem (
7.3
) as shown in
Theorem
2.2
-
b
. On the finite volume
Proposition
3.13
-
a
, implies for

\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\varepsilon,\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|(\mathcal{H}^{\infty}_{t}-\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leqslant T\}}\mathcal{H}^{T}_{t})(\varphi)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{T}^{\infty}|\mathcal{H}^{\infty}_{t}(\varphi)|\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}|(\mathcal{H}^{\infty}_{t}-\mathcal{H}^{T}_{t})(\varphi)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\rho}\langle T\rangle^{1-4\delta}+\langle T\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\langle t\rangle^{-4\delta+\tilde{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}t}_{<\infty}.

Combined with the convergence of
X_{T}^{g}\rightarrow X_{\infty}^{g}
from
Theorem
4.6
and the continuity of
g
, this implies
for any fixed
r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})
,

\hat{J}^{g}_{\infty}(r)=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\hat{J}_{T}^{g}(r).

By the optimality of
r^{T,g}
for
T<\infty
this implies for any
r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})
,

\liminf_{T\rightarrow\infty}\hat{J}_{T}^{g}(r^{T,g})=\liminf_{T\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})}\hat{J}^{g}_{T}(r)\leqslant\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\hat{J}^{g}_{T}(r)=\hat{J}_{\infty}^{g}(r).

(7.9)

From the continuity of
\nabla g
and the convergence of the solution

(Z^{T,g},R^{T,g})\rightarrow(Z^{g},R^{g})
derived in Theorem

4.6
, we immediately get in the
T\to\infty
limit

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|r^{g}_{t}-r_{t}^{T,g}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|Q_{t}(R_{t}^{T,g}-R_{t}^{g})\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-2}\langle T\rangle^{-\varepsilon}\rightarrow 0,

Therefore, by Fatou’s Lemma and the continuity of
g
and
V_{0}
,

\hat{J}_{\infty}^{g}(r^{g})=\mathbb{E}\left[g(X_{\infty}^{g})+V_{0}(X_{0}^{g})+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}^{\infty}_{s}(X^{g}_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{\infty}\|r^{g}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t\right]\leqslant\liminf_{T\rightarrow\infty}\hat{J}_{T}^{g}(r^{T,g}).

(7.10)

Combining (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) we
obtain the missing inequality,

\hat{J}_{\infty}^{g}(r^{g})\leqslant\liminf_{T\rightarrow\infty}\hat{J}_{T}^{g}(r^{T,g})\leqslant\inf_{r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})}\hat{J}_{\infty}^{g}(r).

∎

Remark 7.3
.

The boundedness of the cosine interaction is the reason we have good bounds
over the optimisers to the control problem (
2.8
)
uniformly in
T
. This allows us to bypass the technically more involved

\Gamma
-convergence for the cost functionals
\hat{J}^{T}\rightarrow J
to
remove the small-scale regularisation
T
as was instead necessary
in
[
4
]
in the case of the
\Phi^{4}_{3}

model on a bounded domain.

The variational description for the Laplace transform is now an immediate
consequence of the description in Theorem
7.2
.

Corollary 7.4
.

The variational problem for the Laplace transform (
2.9
) also
holds for
T=\infty
, that is,

-\log\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}(\mathrm{e}^{-g})=\mathcal{W}^{\rho}(g):=\inf_{r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})}\hat{J}_{\infty}^{\rho,g}(r)-\inf_{r\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})}\hat{J}_{\infty}^{\rho,0}(r).

Proof.

From the weak convergence of
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T}\rightarrow\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}
in
H^{-\varepsilon}
,

-\log\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}(\mathrm{e}^{-g})=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}-\log\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T}(\mathrm{e}^{-g})=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{W}_{T}^{\rho}(g)=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{V}_{T}^{V^{\rho}+g}-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{V}_{T}^{V^{\rho}}=\mathcal{W}^{\rho}(g),

where we used (
3.14
) and Theorem
7.2
to
justify the last two equalities.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

It should be emphasised that the change of variables (
7.4
) makes
the extension to
T=\infty
possible: passing to the remainder term
r^{u}=u-z^{u}
allows us to incorporate the singular part
z^{u}
of the control
into the flow equation remainder
\mathcal{H}^{\rho,T}_{s}
while optimising
only over absolutely continuous shifts
r^{u}
. Indeed, while we have
z^{u}\in\mathbb{H}_{T}^{2}(L^{2})
for any
u\in\mathbb{H}_{T}^{2}(L^{2})
, our estimates on

z^{u}
only allow

\|z^{u}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-2\delta},

which is not sufficient to conclude
z^{u}\in\mathbb{H}(L^{2})
unless

\delta>1/2
(
\Leftrightarrow\beta^{2}<4\pi
). In contrast, the
estimates on the optimal FBSDE (see e.g. Lemma
4.1
) suggests
that the remainder
r=z^{u}-u
remains square-integrable for the whole
subcritical regime
\delta>0
(provided of course that an appropriate
approximate solution
V
to the flow equation is used).

Remark 7.6
.

Differentiating (
7.3
) with respect to
the initial value
X_{0}=\varphi
, we obtain a formula for the gradient of
the value function in terms of the solution to the optimal
FBSDE (
4.1
)

\nabla\mathcal{V}^{V+g}(\varphi)=\nabla J^{g}(\bar{u}^{g};\varphi)=(\nabla g+F_{0})(\varphi)+R^{g}_{0}(\varphi).

(7.11)

7.2
Infinite volume

We finally want to remove the restriction to the finite volume. Of course, the
potential will not be meaningful without a spatial cut-off. What saves the
variational problem for the Laplace transform in the infinite volume are the
localisation properties we derived earlier: since the effect of a local
perturbation only has a localised effect on the optimal control by Proposition

4.5
-
c
we are able to show that
the functional

\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g,\rho}(v):=\hat{J}^{g,\rho}(v+\bar{r}^{\rho})-\hat{J}^{0,\rho}(\bar{r}^{\rho}),

stays meaningful in the infinite volume limit, at least if the functional
g

is sufficiently localised and the coupling constant
\bar{\lambda}
is small enough.
This change of variables follows the same idea we used for the finite volume
variational problem: it again allows us to absorb the singular part in a
normalisation while we only optimise along the absolutely continuous directions,
which in this case corresponds to controls in
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2,n})
.

The aim is to show the following.

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
R^{0}
be the backward component of the
solution to the FBSDE (
4.1
) for
g=0
,
\rho=1,T=\infty
and define
\bar{r}:=-QR^{0}
. Then, with

\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g}(v)=\mathbb{E}\left[g(\hat{X}_{\infty}^{\bar{r}+v})+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}_{s},v_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\bar{r}_{s},v_{s}\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right],

the Laplace transform of the infinite volume sine-Gordon measure

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
satisfies the variational problem

\mathcal{W}(g):=-\log\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}(\mathrm{e}^{-g})=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g}(v).

Here, the functional
\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\rho}(\bar{r}_{s},v_{s})
is defined for
any
\rho\preceq 1
in terms of (
3.49
), in
complete analogy to (
3.48
),

\mathcal{H}_{t}^{1}(\bar{r}_{t},v_{t}):=\sum_{\ell=4}^{6}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{1:\ell}h_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell})[{}^{\bar{r}+v}_{t}-{}^{\bar{r}}_{t}](\xi_{1:\ell}),

with
{}^{\bar{r}+v}_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell}):=\exp\left(i\beta\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\sigma_{k}\hat{X}_{t}^{\bar{r}+v}(x_{k})\right)
.

Proof.

Restriction to
\mathbb{D}_{g}
:
Motivated by Proposition

4.5
-
c
, we expect that the
regular part of the control is captured nicely by the domain,

\mathbb{D}_{g}=\mathbb{D}_{g}(C):=\left\{v\in\mathbb{H}_{a}:\;\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2,n}}\mathrm{d}s\leqslant C|g|_{1,2,n}\right\},

(7.12)

provided
C>0
is chosen sufficiently large. We first show convergence of
the restricted variational problem

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{g}^{\rho}(g):=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}(C)}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g,\rho}(v)\overset{\rho\rightarrow 1}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{W}(g):=-\log\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}(\mathrm{e}^{-g})=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g}(v).

We claim that this restriction does not change the finite volume variational
problem, that is for any
\rho\prec 1
,

\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g,\rho}(v)=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{g}^{\rho}(g)=\mathcal{W}^{\rho}(g)=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2})}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g,\rho}(v).

(7.13)

We know from Theorem
7.2
that
\bar{r}^{g,\rho}_{t}=-Q_{t}R_{t}^{g,\rho}
is optimal for the variational
problem (
7.6
), and from Proposition

3.13
-
c
that
\|R_{t}^{g,\rho}-R_{t}^{0,\rho}\|_{L^{2,n}}\lesssim|g|_{1,2,n}
. Thus, for
some constant
C_{g}>0
,

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\overline{r_{s}}^{g,\rho}-\bar{r}_{s}^{\rho}\|_{L^{2,n}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\lesssim\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle s\rangle^{-2}\|R_{t}^{g,\rho}-R_{t}^{0,\rho}\|^{2}_{L^{2,n}}\leqslant C_{g}|g|_{1,2,n}^{2}.

But then
\bar{v}^{g,\rho}=\bar{r}^{g,\rho}-\bar{r}^{\rho}\in\mathbb{D}_{g}
for
C
sufficiently large this implies (
7.13
) for
any
C\geqslant C_{g}
.

Convergence:
We show that uniformly on
\mathbb{D}_{g}
,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g,\rho}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[g(\hat{X}_{\infty}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}+v})+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\rho}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s},v_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s}+v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\rho\rightarrow 1}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}\left[g(\hat{X}_{\infty}^{\bar{r}+v})+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}_{s},v_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle\bar{r}_{s},v_{s}\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\right].

We proceed term by term. Going left to right, we start by estimating

|g(\phi_{1})-g(\phi_{2})|=\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla g(\phi_{1}+\vartheta(\phi_{2}-\phi_{1})))(\phi_{2}-\phi_{1})\mathrm{d}\vartheta\lesssim|g|_{1,2,n}\|\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2,n}}.

The convergence
g(\hat{X}_{\infty}^{\rho,\bar{r}^{\rho}+v})\rightarrow g(\hat{X}_{\infty}^{\bar{r}+v})
then follows from
Lemma
7.8
below. For the remainder term, we write,

|\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}_{s},v_{s})-\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\rho}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s},v_{s})|\leqslant|\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}_{s},v_{s})-\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s},v_{s})|+|\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s},v_{s})-\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\rho}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s},v_{s})|.

For the first term, the definition of
\mathcal{H}^{1}
and the estimates on
the coefficients
h
in (
3.50
) imply,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}_{t},v_{t})-\mathcal{H}_{t}^{1}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{t},v_{t})|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\ell=4}^{6}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{1:\ell}h_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell})[{}^{\bar{r}+v}_{t}-{}^{\bar{r}}_{t}-({}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}+v}_{t}-{}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}}_{t})](\xi_{1:\ell})

\displaystyle\lesssim\interleave h_{t}\interleave(\|\delta_{v}\hat{X}_{t}^{\bar{r}+v}-\delta_{v}\hat{X}_{t}^{\rho,\bar{r}^{\rho}+v}\|_{L^{2,n}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{4}\langle t\rangle^{-4}\|\delta_{v}\hat{X}_{t}^{\bar{r}+v}-\delta_{v}\hat{X}_{t}^{\rho,\bar{r}^{\rho}+v}\|_{L^{2,n}}.

Since
\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{4}\langle t\rangle^{-4}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, the
desired convergence will follow from Lemma
7.8

below. For the second term,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{t},v_{t})-\mathcal{H}_{s}^{\rho}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{t},v_{t})|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\ell=4}^{6}\int\mathrm{d}\xi_{1:\ell}|1-\rho(\xi_{1:\ell})||h_{t}(\xi_{1:\ell})[{}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}+v}_{t}-{}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}}_{t}](\xi_{1:\ell})|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|1-\rho\|_{L^{2,-n}}\interleave h_{t}\interleave\|\hat{X}_{t}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}+v}-\hat{X}_{t}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}}\|_{L^{2,n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|1-\rho\|_{L^{2,-n}}\bar{\lambda}_{t}^{4}\langle t\rangle^{-4}\|v_{t}\|_{L^{2,n}}.

Hence,

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 1}\sup_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}_{s},v_{s})-\mathcal{H}_{s}^{1}(\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s},v_{s})|\mathrm{d}s=0.

Finally, for the quadratic terms, we expand the square to find,

\frac{1}{2}\|\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s}+v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\|v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\langle\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s},v_{s}\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Consequently, for any
v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}
,

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\langle\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s}-\bar{r}_{s},v_{s}\rangle_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\bar{r}^{\rho}_{s}-\bar{r}_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2,-n}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2}\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|v_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2,n}}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{1/2},

which with
\bar{r}_{t}-\bar{r}^{\rho}_{t}=-Q_{t}(R_{t}-R_{t}^{\rho})
and the
estimates on
Q
in Lemma
A.4
and Proposition

4.5
-
c
converges to
0

uniformly on
\mathbb{D}_{g}
.

Recovering the full domain
: Finally, we show that for any
C\geqslant C_{g}
,

\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}(C)}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g}(v)=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g}(v).

Since
\mathbb{D}_{g}(C)\subset\mathbb{D}
, clearly
\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}(C)}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g}(v)\leqslant\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}}\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{g}(v)
. For the reverse inequality, let
\bar{v}\in\mathbb{D}
and let
\bar{C}>\|\bar{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{D}}:=\|\bar{v}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}(L^{2,n})}
so that
\bar{v}\in\hat{\mathbb{D}}_{g}(\bar{C})
and thus by the argument used to show
convergence on
\mathbb{D}_{f}(C)
,

\mathcal{J}^{g}(\bar{v})\geqslant\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}(\bar{C})}\mathcal{J}^{g}(v)=\mathcal{J}^{g}(v^{\ast})=\inf_{v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}(C)}\mathcal{J}^{g}(v).

Taking the infinitum over
\bar{v}\in\mathbb{D}
in the inequality above
then yields the claim.
∎

We still have to supplement the following two convergence results to finish up
the proof of Theorem
7.7
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Using the notation defined
in (
7.5
), it holds uniformly in
v\in\mathbb{D}_{g}
,

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 1}\sup_{s\geqslant 0}\|\hat{X}_{s}^{\bar{r}+v}-\hat{X}_{s}^{\bar{r}^{\rho}+v}\|_{L^{2,-n}}=0.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Proposition
3.13
following the arguments in Proposition

4.5
-
a
and Proposition

4.5
-
d
.
∎

7.3
Large deviations

We apply the variational problem for the Laplace transform just derived in
Theorem
7.7
to show the Laplace principle from
Theorem
1.5
for the limiting measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
. More
precisely, we want to study the family of rescaled measures are formally given
by

\text{``}\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=\Xi_{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}^{-1}\exp(\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{-1}V(\varphi))\mu^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\text{,''}

(7.14)

in the limit
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0
. Here
V(\varphi)=\lambda\int\mathrm{d}x\cos(\beta\varphi(x))
denotes as before the cosine interaction and

\mu^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
is the Gaussian measure with covariance
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}
. Taking the Wick-ordering with respect to
\mu^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
and the obvious
modification to the interpolation
G_{s}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}:=\mathord{\mathchar 126h}G_{s}
, the same
derivation as before yields a description for
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
via
the rescaled FBSDE,

\begin{cases}Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}_{s}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{s}))-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}_{s}R^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s},\\
R^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\mathord{\mathchar 126h}H^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{s})-\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\mathrm{D}F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{s})\dot{G}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}R^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s},\end{cases}

(7.15)

where
F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}=F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h},\infty}
and
F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h},T}
is the approximate
solution to the flow equation (
3.2
) with covariance
G^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}

and the rescaled initial data,

F_{T}^{[1],T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(\varphi):=-\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{-1}\lambda_{0}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\beta^{2}}{2}G_{T}(0)}\sin(\beta\varphi)=-\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{-1}\lambda_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}\beta\sin(\beta\varphi).

At least when
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\in[0,1]
, we have
\bar{\lambda}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}\leqslant\bar{\lambda}_{t}
and the well-posedness of (
7.15
) follows in the exact
same way as the well-posedness of (
4.1
) in
Proposition
4.2
. Moreover, rescaling the analysis of
Theorem
4.6
, we see that also the drift
Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}

has a terminal value with regularity

Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty}\in L^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P};H^{2-\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\beta^{2}/4\pi,-n})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P};H^{2-\beta^{2}/4\pi,-n}).

(7.16)

Thus, the same reasoning as in Theorem
4.6
applies and
can use the solution to the FBSDE (
7.15
) to make the formal
definition (
7.14
) precise. Define the measures

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
as a random shift of the rescaled Gaussian free
field,

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}:=\operatorname{Law}(Z_{\infty}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty}).

Since we are only interested in the limit
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0
, we can limit
our considerations to a small neighbourhood of
0
. This has the advantage
that the measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
will essentially behave like the
sine-Gordon measure with parameter
\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}
(see
also
[
21
, Section C]
): if
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}
is
sufficiently small (say
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}<\mathord{\mathchar 126h}_{0}
where
\beta^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 126h}_{0}<4\pi
),
then, by (
7.16
), the measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
is a
Girsanov shift of the free field. More concretely this means we can carry out
the analysis of (
7.15
) by relying on the convergence of the
Wick-ordered cosine illustrated in (
4.7
), as in the absolutely
continuous first region
\beta^{2}<4\pi
already covered
in
[
9
]
. It only remains to check
that this approach is compatible with our definition of the measures
via (
7.15
). This is in part resolved by the following Lemma.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}<\mathord{\mathchar 126h}_{0}
. The solution
Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}

to (
7.15
) satisfies

Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}=\beta\lambda_{0}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}_{s}\mathbb{E}_{s}[\llbracket\sin(\beta(Z_{\infty}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty}))\rrbracket]=\beta\lambda_{0}I_{t}(\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}),

(7.17)

where
\llbracket\sin(\varphi+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty})\rrbracket
is
defined for any
\varphi\in H^{1,-n}
via (
7.20
) below.

Proof.

Let us first introduce again the approximate FBSDEs with the cut-off
T<\infty
. Then, we know from the definition of
F^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
and
R^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
that

F^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}(X^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s})+R^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}=\mathbb{E}_{s}[\nabla V^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{T}(\beta(Z_{T}^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{t}))].

(7.18)

For
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}<\mathord{\mathchar 126h}_{0}
, it follows from Lemma
A.5
and
the convergence of
Z^{T}_{T}\rightarrow Z_{\infty}
in
\mathbb{H}^{\infty}(L^{\infty})
,

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\|Z_{T}^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}-Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty}\|_{H^{1,-n}}^{2}=0.

Moreover, we can use the trigonometric identities to rewrite

\llbracket\sin(\beta(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{T}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{T}))\rrbracket:=\cos(\beta Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{T})\llbracket\sin(\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{T})\rrbracket+\sin(\beta Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{T})\llbracket\cos(\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{T})\rrbracket,

(7.19)

and similarly for the cosine. By Lemma
C.1
, the Wick-ordered
sine (cosine)
\llbracket\sin(\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{T})\rrbracket

converge in
H^{-1+\varepsilon,-n}
. Thus, the products on the
right-hand side of (
7.19
) stay well-defined in the limit as
T\rightarrow\infty
and consequently
\llbracket\sin(\beta(Z^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{T}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{T}))\rrbracket
and
\llbracket\cos(\beta(Z^{T,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{T}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{T}))\rrbracket
converge in
L^{2}(\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P};H^{-1+\varepsilon,-n})
and almost surely to a well-defined limit
which we denote by

\llbracket\sin(\beta(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty}))\rrbracket:=\cos(\beta Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty})\llbracket\sin(\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty})\rrbracket+\sin(\beta Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty})\llbracket\cos(\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty})\rrbracket,

(7.20)

and respectively

\llbracket\cos(\beta(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty}))\rrbracket:=\cos(\beta Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty})\llbracket\cos(\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty})\rrbracket+\sin(\beta Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty})\llbracket\sin(\beta\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty})\rrbracket.

By uniqueness of the limit, we can pass to the limit
T\rightarrow\infty

in (
7.18
) to conclude

F_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(Z_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{t})+R_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}=-\beta\lambda\mathbb{E}_{t}\llbracket\sin(\beta(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{\infty}))\rrbracket,

which immediately implies (
7.17
).
∎

Applying the same argument as in Lemma
7.9
to the cost
functional
\hat{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
we can undo the change of variables to
the remainder in (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) provided
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}<\mathord{\mathchar 126h}_{0}
. In this case, we obtain the cost functionals

\mathcal{J}^{g,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(w):=\mathbb{E}[g(I_{\infty}(\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}+w)+W^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s})+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}V_{\infty}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(u^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}},w)\mathrm{d}s+\mathcal{E}(w,u^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}})],

(7.21)

where
W^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}=\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W
is the rescaled Brownian motion,

\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}:=Q_{t}\mathord{\mathchar 126h}F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{t})+Q_{t}R_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
is the candidate for optimal control,

V_{\infty}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}(u,w):=\lambda\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x(\llbracket\cos(\beta(I_{\infty}(u+w)+W^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty}))\rrbracket-\llbracket\cos(\beta(I_{\infty}(u)+W^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{\infty}))\rrbracket)(x),

and

\mathcal{E}(w,u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|w_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle w_{s},u_{s}\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathrm{d}s.

Since the functional
\mathcal{J}^{g,\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
depends on
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}
also through the
optimal control
\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}=-Q_{t}\mathord{\mathchar 126h}F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{t})-Q_{t}R_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
, we have to first identify the limit of

\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
before we can find the limiting candidate for

\mathcal{J}^{g,0}
.

Lemma 7.10
.

With
\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}=-Q_{t}\mathord{\mathchar 126h}F^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}(Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{t})-Q_{t}R_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
, it holds that,

\lim_{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}\|_{L^{2,-n}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t=0.

(7.22)

Proof.

We use the linear flow approximation
\tilde{F}:=F^{[1]}
for the
SDE (
7.17
) to obtain again a FBSDE. With
X^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}=Z_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{t}
, this results in the FBSDE,

\begin{cases}Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}_{s}(\lambda_{s}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}\beta\sin(\beta X^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s})-\tilde{R}_{s}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}),\\
\tilde{R}_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s(\lambda_{s}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}})^{2}\beta\cos(\beta X^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s})\dot{G}_{s}\sin\left(\beta(X^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s})\right)-\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{\infty}\lambda_{s}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}\cos(\beta X^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s})\dot{G_{s}}\tilde{R}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}\mathrm{d}s.\end{cases}

Thus, the same arguments as before show using
\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\beta^{2}<4\pi
,

\|R^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}\|_{L^{2,-n}}\lesssim(\bar{\lambda}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t})^{2}\langle t\rangle^{-1}\sup_{s}\|Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}\|_{L^{2,-n}}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}^{2}\sup_{s}\|Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}\|_{L^{2,-n}}.

Using this estimate in the equation for
Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}
,

\displaystyle\|Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}\|_{L^{2,-n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\bar{\lambda}_{s}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}\langle s\rangle^{-2}\|Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{s}\|_{L^{2,-n}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\langle s\rangle^{-2}\|W_{s}\|_{L^{2,-n}}

\displaystyle+\bar{\lambda}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\langle s\rangle^{-2}\sup_{r}\|Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{r}\|_{L^{2,-n}}.

Keeping in mind that

\mathbb{E}\|W_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2,-n}}=G_{s}(0)\lesssim\log(s\vee 1)+1,

we rearrange and take expectation to find for
\bar{\lambda}
sufficiently small,

\mathbb{E}\|Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}\|_{L^{2,-n}}\lesssim\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\langle s\rangle^{-2}\mathbb{E}[\|W_{s}\|^{2}_{L^{2,-n}}]^{1/2}\lesssim\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}\int_{0}^{t}\langle s\rangle^{-2}(\log(s\vee 1)+1)\lesssim\mathord{\mathchar 126h}.

Putting everything together,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\|\bar{u}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2,-n}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{0}\beta\|Q_{t}\sin((\beta Z_{t}^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}^{1/2}W_{t}))\|_{L^{2,-n}}^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\bar{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\langle t\rangle^{-2}(\mathbb{E}\|Z^{\mathord{\mathchar 126h}}_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2,-n}}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\mathbb{E}\|W_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2,-n}})\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\bar{\lambda}\mathord{\mathchar 126h}+\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\langle t\rangle^{-2}\log(t\vee 1)

\displaystyle\lesssim\bar{\lambda}\mathord{\mathchar 126h}.

∎

With the limiting optimal control sorted out, we can now show convergence as

\mathord{\mathchar 126h}\rightarrow 0
in the exact same way as for the case
\beta^{2}<4\pi

and we refer the reader to
[
9
, Section
5]
for details.

8
Osterwalder–Schrader axioms

The Osterwalder–Schrader axioms (OS axioms; for short), as introduced
in
[
34
,
35
]
,
provide sufficient conditions under which the (Euclidean) Schwinger functions
define a relativistic QFT satisfying the Wightman axioms. We only briefly
introduce the aspects that are immediately relevant to our discussion, for a
more detailed exposition we refer to Chapter 6
in
[
26
]
or Section 5
in
[
28
]
. For a Radon measure
\nu

on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we define the associated Schwinger
functions
S^{\nu}_{n}\in(\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))^{\otimes n}
, by

S^{\nu}_{n}(f_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes f_{n}):=\int_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\langle\varphi,f_{1}\rangle\ldots\langle\varphi,f_{n}\rangle\nu(\mathrm{d}\varphi).

(8.1)

We say that
\nu
satisfies the OS axioms, if its associated Schwinger
functions (
8.1
) satisfy the OS-Axioms. We already
reformulate the axioms as conditions on the measures
\nu
instead of the
Schwinger functions above. It is easy to verify that the conditions on
\nu

below imply the OS axioms for the Schwinger
functions (
8.1
).

1.

(
OS1-Regularity
) There is a Schwartz-norm
\|\cdot\|_{S}

and a
\gamma>0
such that

\int_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mathrm{e}^{\gamma\|\varphi\|^{2}_{S}}\nu(\mathrm{d}\varphi)<\infty.

2.

(
OS2-Euclidean invariance
) The measure
\nu
is invariant
under the action of the Euclidean group. More precisely, for any

\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{R},a)\in O(2)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, it holds
that
\nu=\mathcal{G}_{\#}\nu
, where
\mathcal{G}_{\#}\nu(\cdot):=\nu(\mathcal{G}^{-1}(\cdot))
denotes the push forward measure of

\nu
under
\mathcal{G}
.

3.

(
OS3-Reflection positivity
) Define the reflection
\Theta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2},(x_{0},x_{1})\mapsto(-x_{0},x_{1})

along the first coordinate axis. Then, for any exponential observable of the form

\mathcal{O}(\varphi)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\exp\{\langle\varphi,f^{i}\rangle\}
for
f_{i}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with support on

\left\{(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2};\;x^{0}\geqslant 0\right\}
,

\int_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\overline{(\Theta\mathcal{O})(\varphi)}\mathcal{O}(\varphi)\nu(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\geqslant 0.

Here, for
z\in\mathbb{C}
, we denote the complex conjugate by
\bar{z}

and extended the reflection map
\Theta
to functions
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and the observables
\mathcal{O}
via

\Theta f(x_{0},x_{1}):=f(-x_{0},x_{1}),\quad\Theta\mathcal{O}(\varphi):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\exp\{\langle\varphi,\Theta f_{i}\rangle\}.

If the measures
\nu
satisfies the conditions above, then the reconstruction
theorem
[
35
]
(see also
[
26
, Theorem
6.1.3]
) ensures the existence of a
Wightman theory corresponding to the measure
\nu
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Every accumulation point of
(\operatorname{Law}(X^{\rho,T}_{T}))_{\rho,T}
satisfies
OS1
and
OS3
. If moreover,
\bar{\lambda}
is sufficiently small, then also
OS2
holds and the measure is not Gaussian.

The regularity property
for
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
was already shown in
Corollary
4.7
. The remaining claims are proven in the next three sections.

8.1
Euclidean invariance

The Euclidean invariance in this setting is a straightforward consequence of
the uniqueness obtained in Theorem
4.6
.

Proposition 8.2
.

The joint law of
(Z_{\infty},W_{\infty})
is invariant under the action of
the Euclidean group defined by

\mathcal{G}f(x)=f(\mathcal{R}(x-\mathcal{R}^{-1}a))\quad\text{for}\quad\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{R},a)\in O(2)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Proof.

Since the kernels
f^{[\ell]}_{s}
are translation and rotation invariant (see
Remark
3.2
-
c
),

\mathcal{G}F_{s}(X_{s})=F_{s}(\mathcal{G}X_{s}).

Moreover, immediately from the definition of
\dot{G}_{s}
, we have

\mathcal{G}(\dot{G_{s}}f)=\dot{G_{s}}(\mathcal{G}f)
. Therefore, for any

\rho\preceq 1
and
T\leqslant\infty
, the transformed solution

\mathcal{G}X^{\rho,T}
satisfies the equation,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}X_{t}^{\rho,T}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\mathcal{G}\dot{G}_{s}(F^{\rho,T}_{s}(X_{s}^{\rho,T})+R_{s}^{\rho,T})+\mathcal{G}W_{t}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G_{s}}(F^{\mathcal{G}\rho,T}_{s}(\mathcal{G}X_{s}^{\rho,T})+\mathcal{G}R_{s}^{\rho,T})+\mathcal{G}W_{t}.

With the same reasoning,

\mathcal{G}R_{t}^{\rho,T}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}sH^{\mathcal{G}\rho,T}_{s}(\mathcal{G}X_{s}^{\rho,T})-\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{D}F^{\mathcal{G}\rho,T}_{s}(\mathcal{G}X_{s}^{\rho,T})\dot{G}_{s}\mathcal{G}R_{s}^{\rho,T}.

In other words,
(\tilde{X}^{\rho,T},\tilde{R}^{\rho,T}):=\mathcal{G}(X^{\rho,T},R^{\rho,T})
is a solution to

\begin{cases}\tilde{X}^{\rho,T}_{t}=\tilde{W}_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\dot{G}_{s}(F^{\mathcal{G}\rho,T}_{s}(\tilde{X}^{\rho,T}_{s})+\tilde{R}_{s,T}^{\rho})\mathrm{d}s,\\
\tilde{R}_{t}^{\rho,T}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}H^{\mathcal{G}\rho,T}_{s}(\tilde{X}_{s}^{\rho,T})\mathrm{d}s-\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{T}\mathrm{D}F^{\mathcal{G}\rho,T}_{s}(\tilde{X}_{s}^{\rho,T})\dot{G}_{s}\tilde{R}_{s}^{\rho,T}\mathrm{d}s,\end{cases}

where
\tilde{W}:=\mathcal{G}W=\int_{0}^{\cdot}Q_{s}\mathrm{d}(\mathcal{G}B_{s})
is again a Brownian motion with the same covariance as

W
. By the uniqueness of the solution to (
4.1
) (see
Corollary
4.3
) we then have

\operatorname{Law}(\tilde{X}^{\mathcal{G}^{-1}\rho,T},\tilde{W})=\operatorname{Law}(X^{\rho,T},W).

In other words, the joint law is invariant under the action of the Euclidean
group
\mathcal{G}
provided that
\rho=\mathcal{G}\rho
, which holds
only when the weight
\rho
is flat, that is
\rho\propto 1
. In this case,
we have for any
T\leqslant\infty
,

\operatorname{Law}(\tilde{X}^{T}_{T},\tilde{W}_{T})=\operatorname{Law}(\mathcal{G}(X^{T}_{T},W_{T}))=\operatorname{Law}(X^{T}_{T},W_{T}).

∎

8.2
Reflection positivity

To show that
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
is reflection positive, we show that it is the
weak limit of reflection positive measures. We cannot use the approximating
sequence
\nu^{\rho,T}_{\operatorname{SG}}
because the small scale regularisation for

T<\infty
mollifies the measure in all directions and consequently breaks
reflection positivity. Instead, we will construct a new sequence of reflection
positive measures
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\varepsilon,\rho}
such that

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\varepsilon,\rho}\rightarrow\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}
for
any spatial cut-off
\rho\prec 1
. Since weak limits of reflection positive
measures are reflection positive and since
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
is the weak limit
of the finite volume measures
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}
, this will prove the
claim. Throughout this section, we fix a symmetric cut-off
\rho
and suppress
the dependency whenever it does not lead to ambiguities.

To preserve reflection positivity, we cannot mollify in the direction of physical time, and we instead mollify along only one of the coordinate axes. For
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})

supported on
|x|<1
, define the family of mollifiers with

\tilde{\eta}_{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-1}\eta(\cdot\varepsilon^{-1})
on
\mathbb{R}^{1}
and introduce the corresponding mollifiers

\eta_{\varepsilon}=\delta_{0}\otimes\widetilde{\eta_{\varepsilon}}
on

\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, using a variant of Theorem

2.2
-
b
, we can define the measures,

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}=\operatorname{Law}(X^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}_{\infty}),

where

X^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}_{t}=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}^{\varepsilon}_{s}(F^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}_{s}(X^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}_{s})+R^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}_{s})+W^{\varepsilon}_{t}=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}_{s}^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{s}[\nabla V_{T}^{\varepsilon}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon,\rho,T})]+W_{t}^{\varepsilon}.

(8.2)

Here, we defined
G^{\varepsilon}_{t}:=Q_{t}^{\varepsilon}\ast Q^{\varepsilon}_{t}
with
Q_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\eta^{\varepsilon}\ast Q_{t}
, and
obtain
F^{\varepsilon}
and
W^{\varepsilon}
as before by replacing
G
by
its mollification
G^{\varepsilon}
. Then, denoting
\mu^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}:=\operatorname{Law}(W_{T}^{\varepsilon})
, the same argument as in Theorem

2.2
-
b
shows that

\operatorname{Law}(X^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}_{T})\propto\exp\left(-\lambda_{T}^{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\cos(\beta\varphi)\right)\mu^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi),

where
\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\lambda\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}G_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0)}
. The point is now that the additional convolution with

\eta_{\varepsilon}
ensures that the measures
\mu^{\varepsilon}=\mu^{\varepsilon,\infty}
are supported on a function space. Indeed, we
compute for any
\varepsilon>0
,

G^{\varepsilon}_{T}(x)=\int_{0}^{T}\eta_{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G_{s}}(x)\mathrm{d}s\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}\eta_{\varepsilon}\ast\dot{G}_{s}(x)\mathrm{d}s=G^{\varepsilon}_{\infty}(x)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{2}\vee\varepsilon}\right)+r_{\varepsilon}(x),

where
r_{\varepsilon}
is bounded uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and

\varepsilon>0
. In particular, the Wick-ordering with respect to the
Gaussian measure
\mu^{\varepsilon,\rho,T}
is given by

\llbracket\sin(\beta W_{T})\rrbracket=\lambda_{T}^{\varepsilon}\sin(\beta W_{T}),\quad\text{where}\quad\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{T}=\lambda\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}G_{T}^{\varepsilon}(0)}\lesssim\lambda\varepsilon^{-1},

which is not only bounded uniformly in
T
but also converges to a limit at
T=\infty
with
\lambda^{\varepsilon}:=\lambda_{\infty}^{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}G_{\infty}(0)}
. Therefore, the same argument as
used in Theorem
2.2
-
b
implies that for any

\varepsilon>0
, the SDE (
8.2
) is meaningful also for
T=\infty
, with

X^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{t}=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\dot{G}_{s}^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{E}_{s}[\nabla V_{\infty}^{\varepsilon}(X^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{\infty})]+W_{\infty}^{\varepsilon},

(8.3)

has a unique solution for
\bar{\lambda}
sufficiently small. Theorem

2.2
-
b
also implies that the law of

X^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{\infty}
is absolutely continuous with respect to

\mu^{\varepsilon}
and we define

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,\varepsilon}:=\operatorname{Law}(X^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{\infty})\propto\exp\left(-\lambda^{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\rho(x)\cos(\beta\varphi(x))\right)\mu^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}\varphi).

(8.4)

For these measures, reflection positivity will follow directly from the
reflection positivity of
\mu^{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 8.3
.

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
\rho\prec 1
, the measures

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,\varepsilon}
defined in (
8.4
) are
reflection positive.

Proof.

Denote the projection on the positive half-plane
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
by
\pi_{+}
and let again

\Theta f(x_{0},x_{1}):=f(-x_{0},x_{1}),

be the reflection around the first coordinate axis. We first show that the
Gaussian measure
\mu^{\varepsilon}=\operatorname{Law}(W_{\infty}^{\varepsilon})

is reflection positive. Since a Gaussian measure is reflection positive if
and only if its covariance is reflection positive (see e.g.
[
26
, Theorem
6.2.2.]
), it is sufficient to check that
for any function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

G^{\varepsilon}(\pi_{+}f,\Theta\pi_{+}f)=\langle\eta_{\varepsilon}\ast\pi_{+}f,(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}\Theta\eta_{\varepsilon}\ast\pi_{+}f\rangle_{L^{2}}\geqslant 0,

(8.5)

where
G^{\varepsilon}=G^{\varepsilon}_{\infty}
is the covariance

\mu^{\varepsilon}
. Because
\eta_{\varepsilon}
leaves the first
coordinate invariant, the convolution with
\eta_{\varepsilon}
commutes
with the projection
\pi_{+}
. The reflection positivity of
(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}
now implies (
8.5
).

To see that the measures
(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\varepsilon,\rho})_{\varepsilon>0}
defined by (
8.4
) are also
reflection positive, we split the potential between the two half-planes
\{x_{0}\geqslant 0\}
and
\{x_{0}<0\}
, as

V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,\pm}(\varphi):=\lambda^{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\pm}\times\mathbb{R}}\rho(x)\cos(\beta\varphi(x))\mathrm{d}x,

so that

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=\exp(-(V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,+}(\varphi)+V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,-}(\varphi)))\mu^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}\varphi).

For the symmetric cut-off
\rho
, the reflection
\Theta
acts on this
decomposition as
\Theta V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,\pm}=V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,\mp}
. Consequently, we have for any exponential
observable
\mathcal{O}
supported on the positive half plane as defined in

(OS3)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mathcal{O}(\varphi)\Theta\overline{\mathcal{O}(\varphi)}\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\varepsilon,\rho}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mathcal{O}(\varphi)\Theta\mathcal{O}(\varphi)\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,+}(\varphi)}\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,-}(\varphi)}\mu^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\mathcal{O}(\varphi)\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,+}(\varphi)}\Theta(\mathcal{O}(\varphi)\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\varepsilon}^{\rho,+}(\varphi)})\mu^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}\varphi).

Since
V^{\rho,+}
is supported on the positive half plane
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}
, the last integral is non-negative as a result of the
reflection positivity of
\mu^{\varepsilon}
. In other words, for any
symmetric cut-off
\rho\prec 1
, also
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\varepsilon,\rho}
is
reflection positive.
∎

Having established reflection positivity for
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\varepsilon,\rho}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, we want to extract a subsequence which
converges to the desired limiting measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho}
to conclude
this proof. That is, it remains to show that for any
\alpha>0
, there is a
sequence
\varepsilon_{N}\downarrow 0
such that

\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}\|X_{t}^{\varepsilon_{N}}-X_{t}\|^{2}_{H^{-\alpha,-n}}\rightarrow 0.

(8.6)

Adapting the definitions (
3.39
) to the current situation, we see that
with the usual remainder
R^{\varepsilon}
, the FBSDE for the difference is
given by

\begin{cases}X_{t}^{\varepsilon}-X_{t}=-\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s[\dot{G_{s}^{\varepsilon}}(F_{s}^{\varepsilon}(X_{s}^{\varepsilon})+R_{s}^{\varepsilon})-\dot{G}_{s}(F_{s}(X_{s})+R_{s})]+W^{\varepsilon}_{t}-W_{t},\\
R_{t}^{\varepsilon}-R_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{t}\int_{t}^{\infty}(H^{\varepsilon}_{s}(X^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{s})-H_{s}(X_{s})-(\mathrm{D}F_{s}^{\varepsilon,\rho}(X^{\varepsilon,\rho}_{s})\dot{G}^{\varepsilon}_{s}R^{\varepsilon}_{s}-\mathrm{D}F_{s}(X_{s})\dot{G}_{s}R_{s}))\mathrm{d}s.\end{cases}

(8.7)

By definition of
G^{\varepsilon}
, it holds for any
\alpha>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}\|W^{\varepsilon}_{t}-W_{t}\|_{H^{-\alpha,-n}}^{2}=0.

For some subsequence
\varepsilon_{N}\downarrow 0
and any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, Lemma
B.2
combined with
Proposition
3.13
-
a
implies,

\|Q_{s}(F_{s}^{\varepsilon_{N}}-F_{s})(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|Q_{s}(\mathrm{D}F_{s}^{\varepsilon_{N}}-\mathrm{D}F_{s})(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\lesssim N^{-1}\bar{\lambda}_{s}\langle s\rangle^{-1},

and

\|(H^{\varepsilon}_{s}-H_{s})(\varphi)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\lesssim N^{-1}(\bar{\lambda}_{s}\langle s\rangle^{-1})^{4}.

Following the (by now standard) procedure for the FBSDE (
8.7
)
yields (
8.6
) and thus concludes the proof.

Remark 8.4
.

A slight modification of the argument allows to show reflection positivity
for any accumulation point of
(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T})_{T\geqslant 0}
.
Therefore, reflection positivity holds also without the smallness assumption
on the coupling constant
\bar{\lambda}
.

8.3
Non-Gaussianity

We want to show now that for
\bar{\lambda}
small the measure
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
is
non-Gaussian. For any Gaussian measure
\nu
with support on a Hilbert space

\mathcal{H}
with dual pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
, mean
b\in\mathcal{H}^{\ast}
, covariance
C
, Cameron-Martin space

H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\nu)
it holds for
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
,

\frac{1}{2}\|C\psi\|_{H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\nu)}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|C^{1/2}\psi\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=\log\int\exp(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle)\nu(\mathrm{d}\varphi)+\langle b,\psi\rangle.

(8.8)

Showing that the generating function of
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
does not satisfy
(
8.8
) for will therefore yield the claim.

Step 1.
We first show that for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\displaystyle-\log\int\exp(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\|(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1/2}\psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

(8.9)

\displaystyle+\lim_{\rho\to 1}[\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(-(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}\psi)-\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(0)],

where
\mathcal{V}^{\rho}
is the value function as defined in
(
2.8
). By Theorem
4.6
the measure

\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}
on
\mathcal{H}
can be obtained as a weak limit of
measures
\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}^{\rho,T}
that are absolutely continuous with
respect to the Gaussian measure
\mu_{T}=\mathcal{N}(0,G_{T})
.
Combined with the Gaussian tails from Corollary
4.7
this implies convergence of
-\log\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}^{\rho,T}(\exp(-\langle\psi,\cdot\rangle))\to-\log\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}(\exp(-\langle\psi,\cdot\rangle))
by uniform integrability for the functionals
\exp(-\langle\psi,\cdot\rangle)
.
Indeed, for every
L>0
,

\displaystyle\left|\int\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu^{\rho,T}_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)-\int\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\leqslant L\}}\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu^{\rho,T}_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)-\int_{\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\leqslant L\}}\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\int_{\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\geq L\}}\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu^{\rho,T}_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right|+\left|\int_{\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\geq L\}}\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right|.

Regarding the two tail integrals, note that for every
\gamma>0
, there is a
K>0
such that

|-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle|\leqslant\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{\varepsilon,n}}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\leqslant\gamma\left\|\varphi\right\|^{2}_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}+K\left\|\psi\right\|^{2}_{H^{\varepsilon,n}}.

Hence, using Corollary
4.7
, for any
\kappa>0
, we can choose
L>0
sufficiently large so that

\sup_{\rho\prec 1,T\leqslant\infty}\left|\int_{\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\geq L\}}\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu^{\rho,T}_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{3}\kappa.

The first difference converges by weak convergence of
\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}^{\rho,T}
to
\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}
and the boundedness of
\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)
on
\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\leqslant L\}
. Thus, for
L
fixed as above, we may choose
\rho,T
sufficiently close to
1,\infty
so that also

\left|\int_{\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\leqslant L\}}\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu^{\rho,T}_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)-\int_{\{\left\|\varphi\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}\leqslant L\}}\exp(-\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right|\leqslant\frac{\kappa}{3}.

Finally, it remains only to show that also the
\log
converges. To this end, note that by Lemma
4.1
, there are universal constants
K,K_{Z}>0
such that uniformly in
\rho\preceq 1
,
T\in(0,\infty]

\displaystyle\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}^{{\rho,T}}(\exp(-\langle\psi,\cdot\rangle))=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\langle\psi,Z_{T}^{{\rho,T}}\rangle)\exp(-\langle\psi,W_{T}\rangle)]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-K_{Z}\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{\varepsilon,n}}^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\gamma\left\|W_{\infty}\right\|_{H^{-\varepsilon,-n}}^{2})]\geq K>0.

Therefore, the convergence
-\log\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}^{\rho,T}(\exp(-\langle\psi,\cdot\rangle))\to-\log\nu_{\operatorname{sG}}^{\rho,T}(\exp(-\langle\psi,\cdot\rangle))
also follows immediately.
Combined, this allows us to write

\displaystyle-\log\int\exp(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}T\rightarrow\infty\\
\rho\rightarrow 1\end{array}$}}}-\log\left\{\Xi_{T,\rho}^{-1}\int\exp(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle-V_{T}^{T,\rho}(\varphi))\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right\}.

For any
h\in H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\mu)=H^{1}
, by the Cameron–Martin theorem (see e.g.
[
15
, Corollary 2.4.3]
),

\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}(\varphi-h))=\exp\left(\langle\varphi,G_{T}^{-1}h\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle h,G_{T}^{-1}h\rangle\right)\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi).

Thus,

\displaystyle-\log\left\{\Xi_{T,\rho}^{-1}\int\exp(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle-V_{T}^{T,\rho}(\varphi))\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\log\left\{\Xi_{T,\rho}^{-1}\int\exp\left(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle-V_{T}^{T,\rho}(\varphi)+\frac{1}{2}\langle h,G_{T}^{-1}h\rangle-\langle\varphi,G_{T}^{-1}h\rangle\right)\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}(\varphi-h))\right\}

\displaystyle=-\log\left\{\Xi_{T,\rho}^{-1}\int\exp\left(-\langle(\varphi-h),\psi+G_{T}^{-1}h\rangle-V_{T}^{T,\rho}(\varphi+h)+\frac{1}{2}\langle h,G_{T}^{-1}h\rangle\right)\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right\}.

Since
\psi\in H^{-1}
and
G_{\infty}H^{-1}=H^{1}=H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\mu)
,
we may perform a shift along
h_{T}=-G_{T}\psi
to obtain

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\log\int\exp(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}T\rightarrow\infty\\
\rho\rightarrow 1\end{array}$}}}-\log\left\{\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\langle\psi,G_{T}\psi\rangle\right)\Xi_{T,\rho}^{-1}\int\exp(-V_{T}^{T,\rho}(\varphi-G_{T}\psi))\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\|(m^{2}-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}h\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\lim_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}T\rightarrow\infty\\
\rho\rightarrow 1\end{array}$}}}\log\left\{\Xi_{T,\rho}^{-1}\int\exp(-V_{T}^{T,\rho}(\varphi+h_{T}))\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right\}.

For the latter, Theorem
7.7
with
h=-G_{\infty}\psi
implies,

\lim_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}[]{c}T\rightarrow\infty\end{array}$}}}-\log\left\{\Xi_{T,\rho}^{-1}\int\exp(-V_{T}^{T,\rho}(\varphi+h_{T}))\mu_{T}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)\right\}=\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(h)-\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(0),

which yields (
8.9
).

Step 2.

We now show that (
8.8
) implies that
C\psi\in H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\nu)

whenever
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}
.
To this end it is sufficient to show that
\|\psi\|_{H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}})}^{2}\leqslant\infty
whenever
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
.
From Theorem
4.6
, we know that
\operatorname{supp}(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}})\subset H^{-1,-n}
. Therefore, applying (
8.8
) for

\mathcal{H}=H^{-1,-n}
and inserting the estimate from Step 1,

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle C\psi\|_{H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\langle\psi,b\rangle|+|\log\int\exp(-\langle\varphi,\psi\rangle)\nu_{\operatorname{SG}}(\mathrm{d}\varphi)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\psi\|_{H^{-1,-n}}\|b\|_{H^{1,n}}+\frac{1}{2}\|(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\sup_{\rho\leqslant 1}|\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(-G_{\infty}\psi)-\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(0)|.

All the terms except the last one are finite for
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
.
For the difference of the value functions, we use (
7.11
)
combined with Lemma
3.9
and Lemma
4.1
,

\sup_{\rho\leqslant 1}\|\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(h)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant\sup_{\rho\leqslant 1}\|F_{0}^{\rho}(h)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sup_{\rho\leqslant 1}\|R_{0}^{\rho}(h)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1,\qquad h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Then, a simple Taylor expansion combined with the estimates on
F
and
R

\sup_{\rho\leqslant 1}|\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(-G_{\infty}\psi)-\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(0)|\leqslant\sup_{\theta\in[0,1]}\sup_{\rho\leqslant 1}\|\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho,T}(\theta(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}\psi)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|(m^{2}-\Delta)^{-1}\psi\|_{L^{1}}<\infty.

Step 3.
We conclude by showing that (
8.9
) is not
quadratic for
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
, which is valid by Step 2.
It will be more convenient to show that its
gradient is non-linear.
We know from Lemma
3.9
that

\|F_{0}^{\rho}(h)+\rho\lambda\sin(\beta h)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}^{2}\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}.

Combined with
\|R_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\bar{\lambda}^{4}
from Lemma
4.1
, we can
gather all contributions
\ell>1
in a uniformly bounded function
c^{\rho}

satisfying

\sup_{\rho\preceq 1}\sup_{\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\leqslant C}\|c^{\rho}(h)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1,

so that for any fixed
C>0
and
\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\leqslant C
,

\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(h)=-\rho\beta\lambda\sin(\beta h)+\lambda^{2}c^{\rho}(h).

From here, we can see that
\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho}
is not additive. For
example, we may choose
\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{c}\subset H_{\operatorname{CM}}(\nu_{\operatorname{SG}})
such that
\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}\psi(x)=\frac{\pi}{2\beta}
and
\mathbbm{1}_{\{|x|\leqslant 1\}}\tilde{\psi}=\frac{\pi}{4\beta}
. If
\rho\equiv 1
on

B_{1}(0)
, then we verify that on
B_{1}(0)
for
K>0
and
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(\psi+\tilde{\psi})+\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(\psi-\tilde{\psi})-2\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho}(\psi)

(8.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta\lambda\left[\sin\left(\frac{3\pi}{4}\right)+\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right)-2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right]+O(\lambda^{2})

(8.11)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\lambda\left(2-\sqrt{2}\right)-K\lambda^{2}.

(8.12)

For
\bar{\lambda}
sufficiently small, uniformly in
\rho\preceq 1
it holds
that
\lambda\left(2-\sqrt{2}\right)-K\lambda^{2}\geqslant\tilde{K}>0
and we conclude that
\nabla\mathcal{V}^{\rho}
is non-linear for any

\rho\preceq 1
. For
\lambda<0
the the claim follows in the same way.

Appendix A
Heat kernel estimates

This Appendix contains some basic estimates on the heat kernel which we use
throughout as well as some technical proofs which have been postponed.

A.1
General estimates

Lemma A.1
.

With
G
as defined in (
2.3
), there
are uniformly bounded functions
g_{1},g_{2}
and constants
C\geqslant 0

such that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle G_{t}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(t\vee 1)+g_{1}(t),

(A.1)

\displaystyle(G_{\infty}-G_{t})(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(|x|^{-2}t^{-1}\vee 1)+g_{2}(t,x)

(A.2)

\displaystyle\nabla\dot{G}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=C|x|\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t-\frac{t}{4}|x|^{2}},

(A.3)

and consequently

|\dot{G_{t}}(0)-\dot{G}_{t}(x)|\lesssim|x|\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}.

(A.4)

Proof.

The estimate (
A.1
) follows immediately from the

L^{2}
-kernel representation (
2.4
), noting that

G_{t}(0)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/s}=\int_{0}^{1\wedge t}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/s}+\int_{t\wedge 1}^{t\vee 1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/s}=g_{1}(t)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(t\vee 1).

Regarding (
A.2
), we obtain after a substitution with
u=s^{-1}|x|^{-2}
,

\displaystyle(G_{\infty}-G_{t})(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/s}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{s}{4}|x|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t^{-1}|x|^{-2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}|x|^{2}s}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{4s}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1\wedge t^{-1}|x|^{-2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}|x|^{2}s}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{4s}}+\int_{1\wedge t^{-1}|x|^{-2}}^{t^{-1}|x|^{2}\vee 1}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}|x|^{2}s}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{4s}}

\displaystyle=g_{2}(t,x)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\log(t^{-1}|x|^{-2}\vee 1).

Finally, (
A.3
) is a direct computation and (
A.4
)
follows from

\frac{|\dot{G_{t}}(0)-\dot{G}_{t}(x)|}{|x|}\lesssim\sup_{y}|\nabla\dot{G}_{t}(y)|.

Maximising the right-hand side, we see that the maximum is attained at
y=Cs^{-1/2}
for some constant
C
which gives the claim.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

For
{}^{2}<4c
, it holds that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{e}^{-ct|x|^{2}+m\gamma|x|-m^{2}/s}|x|^{2k}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1-k}.

Proof.

We treat the small and large scales separately. For
t>1
,

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{4}|x|^{2}+\gamma m|x|}\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{8}|x|^{2}},

so that the estimate follows as in the unweighed case. To deal with the
large scales, note that for any
c\geqslant 0
the polynomial

p(x):=ct|x|^{2}-m(\gamma-\varepsilon)|x|+\frac{m^{2}}{t},

attains its minimum at
x^{\pm}=\pm\frac{m(\gamma-\varepsilon)}{2ct}
and
p(x)\geqslant p(x^{\pm})\geqslant-\varepsilon^{2}/t

provided
(\gamma-\varepsilon)^{2}<4c
. Therefore, choosing
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small depending on
{}^{2}<4c
, we have

\mathrm{e}^{-ct|x|^{2}+\gamma|x|}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon|x|}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}/t},

and thus

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\frac{1}{4\pi s}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{4}|x|^{2}+\gamma|x|}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t}\lesssim\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}y\frac{1}{4\pi}t^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon|x|}\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon^{2}/t}<\infty.

∎

Lemma A.3
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+},x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
c\in(0,1)
, we have

|\dot{G}_{t}(x)-\dot{G}_{t}(y)|\mathrm{e}^{-c\frac{t}{4}|x-y|^{2}}\lesssim|x-y|(|x|+|x-y|)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{c}{2}\frac{t}{4}|x|^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t}.

The same estimate holds for
\dot{G}_{t}
replaced by
t^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/2t}Q_{t}
.

Proof.

We start by rewriting the difference as

\dot{G}_{t}(x)-\dot{G_{t}}(y)=(y-x)\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\nabla\dot{G}_{t}(x-\vartheta(x-y)).

For any
z=x-\vartheta(x-y)
and
\vartheta\in[0,1]
, we have

\frac{1}{2}|x|^{2}\leqslant|x-z|^{2}+|z|^{2}\leqslant\vartheta^{2}|x-y|^{2}+|z|^{2}
. Combined with (
A.3
) this means

|\nabla\dot{G}_{t}(z)|\leqslant C|z|\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{4}|z|^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t}\leqslant C|z|\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{c}{2}\frac{t}{4}|x|^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{c\frac{t}{4}|x-y|^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t},

and consequently,

|\nabla\dot{G}_{t}(z)|\mathrm{e}^{-c\frac{t}{4}|x-y|^{2}}\leqslant C|z|\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{c}{2}|x|^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t}\leqslant C(|x|+|x-y|)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{c}{2}\frac{t}{4}|x|^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-m^{2}/t}.

The estimate on
Q
follows in the exact same way, only replacing the
estimate on the gradient by

|\nabla Q_{t}(x)|\lesssim t|x|\mathrm{e}^{-2t|x|^{2}-m^{2}/2t}.

∎

Lemma A.4
.

For
k\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha>0
, and
\gamma\in(-1,1)
, consider the weight
w(x)=\langle x\rangle^{k}
or
w(x)=\exp(\gamma m|x|)
. Then, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
u\in L^{p}(w)
,

\displaystyle\||x|^{2\alpha/p}Q_{t}\|_{L^{p}(w)}=\langle t\rangle^{-1/p-\alpha/p},

\displaystyle\|Q_{t}u\|_{L^{p}(w)}\lesssim\langle t\rangle^{-1/p}\|u\|_{L^{1}(w)},

(A.5)

\displaystyle\||x|^{2\alpha/p}\dot{G}_{t}\|_{L^{p}(w)}=\langle t\rangle^{-1-1/p-\alpha/p},
